Sharp Hölder Regularity for Nirenberg’s Complex Frobenius Theorem
Liding Yao

Abstract
Nirenberg’s famous complex Frobenius theorem gives necessary and sufficient conditions on a locally integrable structure for when the manifold is locally diffeomorphic to ℝ r × ℂ m × ℝ N − r − 2 ​ m \mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m} through a coordinate chart F F in such a way that the structure is locally spanned by F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ t 1 , … , F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ t r , F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ z 1 , … , F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ z m F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}} , where we have given ℝ r × ℂ m × ℝ N − r − 2 ​ m \mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m} coordinates ( t , z , s ) (t,z,s) . In this paper, we give the optimal Hölder-Zygmund regularity for the coordinate charts which achieve this realization. Namely, if the structure has Hölder-Zygmund regularity of order α > 1 \alpha>1 , then the coordinate chart F F that maps to ℝ r × ℂ m × ℝ N − r − 2 ​ m \mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m} may be taken to have Hölder-Zygmund regularity of order α \alpha , and this is sharp. Furthermore, we can choose this F F in such a way that the vector fields F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ t 1 , … , F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ t r , F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ z 1 , … , F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ z m F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}} on the original manifold have Hölder-Zygmund regularity of order α − ε \alpha-\varepsilon for every ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , and we give an example to show that the regularity for F ∗ ​ ∂ ∂ z F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z} is optimal.



Keywords —
Complex Frobenius structures, Newlander-Nirenberg theorem, Bi-parameter Hölder spaces, Malgrange’s factorizations.

1
Introduction

For
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
, let
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
be the class of Hölder-Zygmund functions
1
1

1

For non-integer exponents, the Hölder-Zygmund space agrees with the classical Hölder space. When
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have
C^{\alpha-1,1}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. See Remark
2.3
.
of order
\alpha
(See Definitions
1.8
). Let
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\varepsilon}
. And we denote
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}=C^{\infty}
as the class of smooth functions.

Given a smooth manifold
\mathfrak{M}
, a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure
on
\mathfrak{M}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex tangent subbundle
2
2

2

For (real or complex) linear spaces
V,W
, we use
V\leq W
as saying that
V
is a (real or complex) subspace of
W
. And we use
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
as saying
\mathcal{S}
is a complex subbundle of
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
.

\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
, such that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is also a complex tangential subbundle, and both
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
satisfy the
involutivity condition
, namely

\forall X,Y\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})\Rightarrow[X,Y]\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}),\quad\text{and }\forall X,Y\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})\Rightarrow[X,Y]\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}).

See Definition
3.10
.

The complex Frobenius theorem was first introduced by Louis Nirenberg. It is a generalization of the real Frobenius theorem and the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem.

Proposition 1.0

(L. Nirenberg
[
Nir57
]
)
.

Let
r,m,N
be nonnegative integers satisfying
r+2m\leq N
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
N
-dimensional smooth manifold and let
\mathcal{S}
be a smooth complex Frobenius structure on
\mathfrak{M}
, such that

\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
.
Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a smooth coordinate system
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}_{s}
near
p
, such that
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
is spanned by the smooth vector fields
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

Here
(t,z,s)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m},s^{1},\dots,s^{N-r-2m})
is the standard coordinate system for
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}
. All the ranks and spans are in the sense of complex subspaces, not as real subspaces.

1.1
A first glimpse to the main results

In this paper we find such a coordinate chart
F
with optimal Hölder-Zygmund regularity, when the complex Frobenius structure
\mathcal{S}
has low smoothness. To obtain the best results, we compute the regularities
F^{\prime}
,
F^{\prime\prime}
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}
separately, instead of merely giving a single regularity result for
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime})
.

We say a complex tangent subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, if locally it is spanned by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields (Definitions
3.5
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
N
-dimensional smooth manifold, and let
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure such that
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
.

Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-coordinate system
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}_{s}
near
p
, such that
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

In fact
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. Moreover
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
-complex vector fields in
U
.

In this theorem
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
are all optimal, in the sense that given
\alpha>1
, we have examples of complex Frobenius structures
\mathcal{S}
, such that if
F
is a
C^{1}
-chart representing
\mathcal{S}
, then
F^{\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
, and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, respectively. See Section
7
. As a corollary, the regularity statement of our coordinate chart
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
is optimal.

The subbundle
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
(if they have constant ranks, see Remark
3.13

(ii)
) are always at least as regular as the subbundle
\mathcal{S}
. However, it is possible that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
have better regularity than
\mathcal{S}
. See Remark
3.13

(iii)
.
When
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is more regular than
\mathcal{S}
, we have an improvement of Theorem
1.1
:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(1,\infty)
satisfy
\alpha+1\leq\beta
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
N
-dimensional smooth manifold. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure, such that
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-complex tangential subbundle with complex rank
r
.

Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-coordinate system
F:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}
near
p
, such that in addition to the consequences in Theorem
1.1
, we have that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-vector fields on
U
.

There is another characterization for representing the complex Frobenius structure using the inverse of coordinate charts, the so-called ‘‘regular parameterization’’. Let
3
3

3

We use
f^{\mathrm{Inv}}
instead of
f^{-1}
to refer the inverse map of
f
, if it is bijective.

\Phi=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}:F(U)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathfrak{M}
be a such parameterization. For
F
we work with the regularities of
F^{\prime}
,
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}
, while for
\Phi
we work with that of
[t\mapsto\Phi(t,z_{0},s_{0})]
,
[z\mapsto\Phi(t_{0},z,s_{0})]
and
[s\mapsto\Phi(t_{0},z_{0},s)]
, for any fixed
t_{0},z_{0},s_{0}
.

For a general version of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, which includes the estimates for
\Phi
, see Theorems
6.1
and
6.2
.

To see that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
is sharp; in Section
7.2
we construct an example that meets the following:

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\alpha>1
. There is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, such that
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=1
,
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})=2
, and there does not exist a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart
F:U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{3})
.

See Theorem
7.12
for a general version.

1.2
Historical remarks

There are many previous discussions of complex Frobenius theorem in the nonsmooth setting.
Nijenhuis & Woolf
[
NW63
]
studied Newlander-Nirenberg theorem with nonsmooth parameters, which is a special case of nonsmooth complex Frobenius theorem.

Later Hill & Taylor
[
HT07
]
studied nonsmooth complex Frobenius structures. They assumed the complex Frobenius structure to be at least
C^{1,1}
in order to achieve the condition called “Hypothesis V” in their paper.

Recently, Gong
[
Gon20
]
proved a better estimate for complex Frobenius structure. He showed that for a non-integer
\alpha>1
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
, locally one can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
-coordinate chart
F:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
span
\mathcal{S}
. Though he did not have the sharp result, the statement
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
is endpoint sharp (up to a loss of
\varepsilon>0
derivatives).
The proof technique in his paper is quite different for ours. He used homotopy formulae with Nash-Moser iterations.

Our result is stronger because we have not only the optimal regularity of the coordinate chart that
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, but also the optimal regularity for other ingredients like the components of the coordinates and the coordinate vector fields. See Section
7
.

As the origin of the complex Frobenius theorem, the sharp estimate to Newlander-Nirenberg is due to Malgrange
[
Mal69
]
. He proved that for
\alpha>1
one can always obtain a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
local complex coordinate system for a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
integrable almost complex structure. His argument also works for
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha\leq 1
, see
[
HT03
]
. We remark that we cannot replace
(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha},\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1})
by
(C^{k},C^{k+1})
, see
[
Yao21
]
.

Later Street used Malgrange’s method and obtained a sharp estimate for elliptic structure.

Proposition 1.4

(
[
Str20
]
, Sharp estimate for elliptic structures)
.

Let
\alpha>1
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold, and let
r,m
be integers satisfying
\dim\mathfrak{M}=r+2m
. Endow
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
with standard coordinate system
(t,z)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m})
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-elliptic structure of rank
r+m
. Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there exists a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate system
F:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
near
p
, such that
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

Note that in Proposition
1.4
we have
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
because
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
. However in a more general setting (Theorem
1.1
), due to the influence of the parameters, we only have
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
. Both results for
F
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are sharp.

1.3
Idea of the proof

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a (smooth) manifold. In this section we use the following terminologies:

Definition 1.5
.

We call a
C^{1}
complex tangent subbundle
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is
involutive
, if for every
C^{1}
-vector fields
X,Y
that are sections of
\mathcal{S}
, the Lie bracket
[X,Y]
is a
C^{0}
-section of
\mathcal{S}
. (See also Definition
3.7
.)

•

An
elliptic structure
on
\mathfrak{M}
is an involutive complex subbundle
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}=\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

A
complex structure
4
4

4

In this paper we use “complex structure” to refer the almost complex structure satisfying involutivity condition. See also
[
BCH08
, Section I.8]
.
on
\mathfrak{M}
is an involutive complex subbundle
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}=\mathcal{S}\oplus\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

An
essentially real structure
is a complex subbundle
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that
\mathcal{S}=\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

Here
\bar{\mathcal{S}}=\{(p,u-iv):p\in\mathfrak{M},\ u,v\in T_{p}\mathfrak{M},\ u+iv\in\mathcal{S}_{p}\}
is the complex conjugate subbundle of
\mathcal{S}
.

To prove Theorem
1.1
, notice that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is an essentially real structure. By applying the real Frobenius theorem, we get a foliation of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifolds, such that by restricted to each leaf
\mathcal{S}
becomes an elliptic structure.
The theorem is proved once we have the estimate of the coordinate system and the coordinate vector fields for elliptic structures with parameters:

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
be two smooth manifolds such that
\dim\mathfrak{M}=r+2m
and
\dim\mathfrak{N}=q
. Let
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
be a rank-
(r+m)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-involutive subbundle such that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
. Then for any
(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-coordinate chart
F:U\times V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
such that
\mathcal{S}|_{U\times V}
is spanned by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
-vector fields
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

See Theorem
5.1
for a more general version, where we consider the case of mixed regularity
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
with
0<\beta\leq\alpha+1
(see Definitions
2.10
,
2.15
,
3.2
,
3.6
and
3.7
). Theorem
5.1
implies Proposition
1.4
which can be viewed as the “parameter free” case. See also Remarks
5.2
,
5.3
and
5.4
.

The idea is based on Malgrange’s factorization method for the sharp Newlander-Nirenberg theorem, whose sketch can be found in, for example
[
BCH08
, Page 47]
and
[
HT03
, Section 3]
. Malgrange’s idea is to find an intermediate coordinate change
H
such that the generators for
H_{*}\mathcal{S}
satisfy an elliptic PDE system. Using the PDE we can show that
H_{*}\mathcal{S}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-family of real-analytic elliptic structures. Finally we construct another coordinate change
G
using holomorphic Frobenius theorem, now with parameter, so that
G_{*}H_{*}\mathcal{S}
is the span of
\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
. Thus
F=G\circ H
is the desired coordinate chart.

See Section
5.1
for a detailed sketch to the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Note that in Theorem
1.6
we work on the elliptic structures rather than the complex structures. In this way we do not have to pick generators for
\mathcal{S}
that satisfy the “Hypothesis V” in
[
HT07
]
.

For the proof of Theorem
1.2
, we follow Nirenberg’s original proof by applying real Frobenius theorem twice on the essentially real structures
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, see
[
BCH08
, the proof of Theorem I.12.1]
. This ends up with a family of complex structures. We complete the proof by applying Theorem
1.6
with the special case
r=0
.

To get the optimal Hölder-Zygmund regularity during this reduction, we combine the two real Frobenius theorems into one step. We call it the
bi-layer Frobenius theorem
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a smooth manifold with dimension
(r+m+q)
. Let
\alpha,\beta>1
and let
\mathcal{V}\leq\mathcal{E}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
be two involutive real subbundles such that
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
,
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{V}=r
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{E}=r+m
.

Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}
-coordinate system
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
near
p
, such that
\mathcal{V}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
and
\mathcal{E}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}
.

Moreover for their regularities we have
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha-1,\beta)}
.

See Theorem
3.27
for a more general version. In Theorem
1.2
we take
\alpha+1\geq\beta
,
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{S}\cap T\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})\cap T\mathfrak{M}
.

Unlike the sharp Newlander-Nirenberg theorem or Street’s result where
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields, in our result we show that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
is optimal.

One reason for the regularity loss comes from solving elliptic pdes. Say we have a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle for some
\alpha>0
. In the first step of Malgrange’s proof of the sharp Newlander-Nirenberg theorem we need to solve an elliptic pde system for
f=f(x)
that has the following form:

\Delta f=\phi(f,\nabla f,\nabla^{2}f,a,\nabla a).

(1.1)

Here
a=a(x)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
is the vector-valued coefficient that comes from the subbundle, and
\phi
is a rational function which is defined and small when
f
and
a
are both small.

Since Laplace’s equation gains two derivatives in Hölder-Zygmund spaces, we can find a
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
solving (
1.1
). This is why we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate system for a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-integrable almost complex structure. In particular the coordinate vector fields are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
because
\nabla f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

When we do estimate on the complex Frobenius structure, we need to solve a pde system that has similar expression to (
1.1
) but with a parameter: we need
f=f(x,s)
for the system
\Delta_{x}f=\phi(f,\nabla_{x}f,a,\nabla_{x}a)
with
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-coefficient
a=a(x,s)
.

We need to introduce some suitable function spaces that capture the property that the pde system “gains 2 derivatives on
x
-variable”. Note that by comparing to
a(x,s)
, the function
\phi(f,\nabla_{x}f,a,\nabla_{x}a)
lose one derivative in
x
, and by taking inverse Laplacian,
f
should gain 1 derivative in
x
. Thus we can ask what is the good function space
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{X}_{x,s}
to work on, such that if
a\in\mathscr{X}_{x,s}
then
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{X}_{x,s}
.

Unfortunately the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
does not satisfy our need. When
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
we may not find a solution
f
such that
\nabla f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
. This is roughly because the
x
-variable Riesz transform (say
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
s\in V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
)

R_{j}f(x,s):=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{n+1}{2})}{\pi^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B^{n}(0,\varepsilon)}\frac{y_{j}-x_{j}}{|y-x|^{n+1}}f(y,s)dy,\quad j=1,\dots,n,

does not map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times V)
into itself. That is why
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
is the best one we can get.

To see that why
R_{j}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}\not\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
holds, we consider the bi-parameter decomposition
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}
(see Lemma
2.12
): we have
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}}\approx\sup_{s}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}}+\sup_{x}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}}
. We see that
R_{j}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}
is still bounded because Riesz transforms are bounded in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-spaces. However
R_{j}:L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}\not\to L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}
is not bounded because Riesz transforms are unbounded on
L^{\infty}
-spaces.

To construct the example of complex Frobenius structure such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, we follow a similar but more sophisticated construction to
[
Yao21
]
. See Propositions
7.3
and
7.9
in Section
7.2
.

1.4
Notations and organizations

We use
distributions
for generalized functions, rather than tangential subbundles on manifolds.

Given a bijection
f:U\to V
, we use
f^{\mathrm{Inv}}:V\to U
as the inverse function of
f
. We do not use the notion
f^{-1}
in order to reduce confusion when
(\cdot)^{-1}
is used for inverting a matrix.

We use
A\Subset B
to denote that
A
is a relatively compact subset of
B
.

By
A\lesssim B
we mean that
A\leq CB
where
C
is a constant independent of
A,B
. We use
A\approx B
for “
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
”. And we use
A\lesssim_{x}B
to emphasize the constant dependence on quantity
x
.

We use
\mathbb{B}^{n}=B^{n}(0,1)
as the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r\mathbb{B}^{n}=B^{n}(0,r)
for
r>0
. We use a complex cone
\mathbb{H}^{n}:=\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},4|y|<1-|x|\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

For functions
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
,
g:V\to\mathbb{R}
, we use the tensor notation
f\otimes g:U\times V\to\mathbb{R}
as
(f\otimes g)(x,y):=f(x)g(y)
.

For the index of Hölder regularity, we use the convention
\infty=\infty+1=\infty-1=\infty+=\infty-=\frac{\infty}{2}
.

We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
the class of Hölder-Zygmund functions:

Definition 1.8
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\infty]
, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a convex open subset and let
X=\mathbb{R}^{m}
or
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. The space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;X)
of bounded (
X
vector-valued) Hölder-Zygmund functions on
\Omega
, is given recursively as follows:

(i)

For
0<\alpha<1
:
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;X)
consists of those continuous functions
f:\Omega\to X
such that:

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;X)}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|_{X}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y\end{subarray}}|x-y|^{-\alpha}|f(x)-f(y)|_{X}<\infty.

(ii)

For
\alpha=1
:
\mathscr{C}^{1}(\Omega;X)
consists of those continuous functions
f:\Omega\to X
such that:

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1}(\Omega;X)}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|_{X}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y\end{subarray}}|x-y|^{-1}|\frac{f(x)+f(y)}{2}-f(\frac{x+y}{2})|_{X}<\infty.

(iii)

For
\alpha>1
:
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;X)
consists of
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega;X)
such that the following norm is finite:

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;X)}:=\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega;X)}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\partial_{j}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega;X)}.

(iv)

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\Omega;X):=\bigcap_{\beta>0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega;X)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega;X):=\bigcap_{\beta<\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega;X)
, both endowed with the standard limit Fréchet topologies. That is, we say
f_{k}\to f_{0}
in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
if
f_{k}\to f_{0}
in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
for all
\beta<\alpha
.

(v)

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}(\Omega):=\bigcup_{\beta>\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)
, whose topology is used in the paper.

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
for
\beta\in\{\alpha,\alpha-,\alpha+:\alpha>0\}\cup\{\infty\}
.

We use
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)}
either when
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is a function or when the codomain of
f
is clear. And we use
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
when both the domain and the codomain are clear.

We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;X)
for
\alpha\leq 0
in Definition
2.4
using the Besov space
\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{x}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{s}
be two convex open subsets. For
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty]
, we denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)
the space of functions on
U\times V
that is bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
in
x
and bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
in
s
separately, see Definition
2.10
. For
\alpha,\beta\in(-\infty,\infty]
we denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
the space of functions on
U\times V
that is bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
in
x
and bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
in
s
simultaneously, see Definition
2.6
. In most of the cases we work on the spaces like
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U,V)
.

Given a coordinate chart
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
, we write
dx:=[dx^{1},\dots,dx^{n}]
as a row vector and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}:=\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{x^{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\partial_{x^{n}}\end{bmatrix}
as a column vector. We may use
X
sometimes as a single vector field, and sometimes as a (column) collection of vector fields
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r}]^{\top}
. We use these conventions when there is no ambiguity.

In a mixed real and complex domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
with standard (real and complex) coordinate system
(t,z)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m})
, for a function
f
on
\Omega
, we use
\nabla f,\nabla_{z}f,\partial_{z}f
as functions taking values in column vectors, which have rows
r+2m
,
2m
and
m
respectively as follows,

\begin{gathered}\textstyle\nabla f=\nabla_{t,z}f=\partial_{t,z,\bar{z}}f=\big[\frac{\partial f}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial t^{r}},\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{m}},\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}\big]^{\top},\\
\textstyle\nabla_{z}f=\partial_{z,\bar{z}}f=\big[\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{m}},\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}\big]^{\top},\qquad\partial_{z}f=\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}=\big[\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{m}}\big]^{\top}.\end{gathered}

(1.2)

For a map
F:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
, we usually use the notation
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})
where
F^{\prime}:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
F^{\prime\prime}:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
are the corresponding components of
F
.

The paper is organized as follows:

In Section
2
we give discussions of Hölder-Zygmund spaces in the bi-parameter setting.
We prove some results for multiplications and compositions of bi-parameter Hölder-Zygmund spaces in Sections
2.2
and
2.3
respectively. These results will be used in Section
4
.

In Section
3
we review the involutive structures and ODE flows. We prove the bi-layer Frobenius theorem Theorem
1.7
(see Theorem
3.27
) and a version of holomorphic Frobenius theorem (Proposition
3.31
) in Section
3.3
. Proposition
3.31
will be used in the last step of Malgrange’s factorization in Section
5.1
.

In Section
4
we prove two results for the nonlinear elliptic PDE system with parameters: one for existence (Section
4.1
) and one for interior real-analyticity (Section
4.2
).

In Section
5
we state and prove the general version of the key result Theorem
1.6
(Theorem
5.1
). We first sketch the idea using Malgrange’s method in Section
5.1
and then prove them in Section
5.2
. To get a desired coordinate chart, we first pick some good generators for the given family of elliptic structures. Then we use the existence result in Section
4.1
to construct an coordinate change such that in the new coordinates the generators satisfy some extra conditions. By the real-analyticity result in Section
4.2
such generators admit a holomorphic extension to a complex domain. Finally, we use the holomorphic Frobenius theorem to complete the proof of Theorem
1.6
. We finish the proof of Theorem
5.1
in Section
5.3
.

In Section
6
we complete the proof of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
(see Theorems
6.1
and
6.2
).

In Section
7
we explain which parts of the results in the main theorems are sharp. For the sharpness of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
we give a complete construction of the complex Frobenius structure (Propositions
7.3
and
7.9
) and give the proof of Theorem
1.3
in Section
7.2
(see Theorems
7.11
and
7.12
).

In Section
B
we include a result of the holomorphic extension for a right inverse Laplacian from
\mathbb{B}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{H}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
(Proposition
4.8
) based on
[
Mor58
]
.

2
The Bi-parameter Hölder-Zygmund Structures

In this section we set up some bi-parameter Hölder-Zygmund spaces that are used to discuss the Hölder regularity of some geometric objects. The results in this section are mostly for technical purposes.

2.1
Hölder-Zygmund spaces of single and bi-parameters

Recall the classical (single-parameter) Hölder-Zygmund spaces in Definition
1.8
. In applications we require the Littlewood-Paley characterizations for Hölder-Zygmund spaces.

Definition 2.1
.

A (Fourier) dyadic resolution of unity for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a sequence of real-valued Schwartz functions
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(a)

The Fourier transform
\hat{\phi}_{0}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(x)e^{-2\pi ix\xi}dx
satisfies
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{0}\subset\{|\xi|<2\}
and
\hat{\phi}_{0}\big|_{\{|\xi|<1\}}\equiv 1
.

(b)

For
j\geq 1
,
\phi_{j}(x):=2^{nj}\phi_{0}(2^{j}x)-2^{n(j-1)}\phi_{0}(2^{j-1}x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Immediately we see that

\operatorname{supp}\phi_{0}\subset\{|\xi|<2\},\quad\operatorname{supp}\phi_{j}\subset\{2^{j-1}<|\xi|<2^{j+1}\},\quad j\geq 1.

(2.1)

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution.

(i)

f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
if and only if
\phi_{j}\ast f\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for every
j\geq 0
and
\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
. Moreover there is a
C=C(\phi,\alpha)>0
that does not depend on
f
such that

\textstyle C^{-1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\forall f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(ii)

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded convex open set with smooth boundary. Then
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
has an equivalent norm

f\mapsto\inf\{\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}:\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\tilde{f}\big|_{\Omega}=f\}.

(iii)

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be either the whole space or a bounded convex open set with smooth boundary. Suppose
0<\alpha<2
, then
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
has an equivalent norm

\textstyle f\mapsto\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|+\sup_{x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y}\big|\frac{f(x)+f(y)}{2}-f\big(\frac{x+y}{2}\big)\big|\cdot|x-y|^{-\alpha}.

See
[
Tri10
, Chapters 2.2.2, 2.5.7 and 3.4.2]
for the proof.

Remark 2.3
.

Using Lemma
2.2
and Definition
1.8
we know that for positive noninteger value, the Hölder-Zygmund spaces coincide with the classical Hölder spaces, that is
\mathscr{C}^{m+s}(\Omega)=C^{m,s}(\Omega)
for
m\geq 0
,
0<s<1
. However for integer cases
C^{m}(\Omega)\subsetneq C^{m-1,1}(\Omega)\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{m}(\Omega)
.

Following the notations in
[
Tri10
, Definition 2.3.1/2(i)]
for
\alpha>0
the Hölder-Zygmund spaces
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
are indeed the Besov spaces
\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
. Thus it is natural to define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-spaces for
\alpha\leq 0
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\alpha\leq 0
, let
X=\mathbb{R}^{m}
or
\mathbb{C}^{m}
, and let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution of unity in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}:=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}
. More precisely,
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};X)
is the space of all temper distributions
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};X)
such that

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};X)}:=\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};X)}<\infty.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded convex domain, we define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega):=\{\tilde{f}|_{\Omega}:\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\}
with norm

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;X)}:=\inf\{\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};X)}:\tilde{f}|_{\Omega}=f\}.

The
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-norms depend on
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
, while the spaces themselves are not. See
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.3.2]
.

We use Littlewood-Paley decomposition along different variables to define bi-parameter Zygmund structures.

Notation 2.5
.

For functions
f(x,s)
and
\rho(x)
, we use
\ast_{x}
as the convolution acting on
x
-variable:

\rho\ast_{x}f(x,s)=(\rho\ast f(\cdot,s))(x).

Note that if
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s})
and
\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\rho\ast_{x}f
is a well-defined tempered distribution in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
, since
\rho\ast_{x}f=(\rho\otimes\delta_{0^{q}})\ast f
. Here
\delta_{0^{q}}
is the Direc delta measure of the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
and
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
be the coordinate system of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
respectively. Let
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\psi=(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
be dyadic resolutions for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

(i)

We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})=L^{\infty}_{s}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{q},\mathbb{R}^{n})
to be the set of all
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
such that
\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
for every
j\geq 0
and

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}=\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})_{\phi}}:=\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}<\infty.

(ii)

We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{q})
to be the set of all
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
such that

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}=\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})_{\phi,\psi}}:=\sup_{j,k\geq 0}2^{j\alpha+k\beta}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}(\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}<\infty.

(iii)

For
\mathscr{X}\in\{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s},\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\}
, let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two open sets, define
\mathscr{X}(U_{x},V_{s})
by

\textstyle\mathscr{X}(U,V):=\{\tilde{f}|_{U\times V}:\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\},\quad\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}(U,V)}:=\inf_{\tilde{f}|_{U\times V}=f}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}.

Let
\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}
be the function classes in
(i)
or
(ii)
, we have convention

\mathscr{X}\cap\mathscr{Y}(U,V):=\mathscr{X}(U,V)\cap\mathscr{Y}(U,V),\quad\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}\cap\mathscr{Y}(U,V)}:=\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}}+\|f\|_{\mathscr{Y}}.

For vector valued functions, we use
\mathscr{X}\cap\mathscr{Y}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{m})
as the spaces of all vector valued functions (or distributions)
f=(f^{1},\dots,f^{m}):U\times V\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
f^{j}\in\mathscr{X}\cap\mathscr{Y}(U,V)
for all
j=1,\dots,m
. And we use

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}\cap\mathscr{Y}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{m})}=\sup_{1\leq j\leq m}\|f^{j}\|_{\mathscr{X}\cap\mathscr{Y}(U,V)}.

We may omit the variables, the domains and the codomains in the norms when they are clear.

Remark 2.7
.

(i)

By Lemma
2.2

(i)
and
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.3.2]
, different choices of
(\phi_{j})
and
(\psi_{k})
result to the equivalent norms. We would use the norm notation
\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}(U,V)}
through fixing a choice of
(\phi,\psi)
implicitly.

(ii)

We have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(U_{x},V_{s})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}(V_{s},U_{x})
. This is due to the fact that

\phi_{j}\ast_{x}(\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f)=\psi_{k}\ast_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f)=(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast f,\quad\text{where }(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})(x,s)=\phi_{j}(x)\psi_{k}(s).

(2.2)

(iii)

When
\alpha>0
and when
U,V
are smooth domains, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V))
in the sense that we take
X=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)
in Definition
1.8
. We leave the proof to readers.

(iv)

However
L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U,V)
does not coincide with the vector-valued space
L^{\infty}(V;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U))
. In classical definitions
L^{\infty}(U;X)
is the set of all
X
-valued strong measurable function (quotiented by almost everywhere) which is essentially bounded. Such functions in
L^{\infty}(U;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V))
must be
almost everywhere separable valued
. But
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
is
not
a separable space. So
L^{\infty}(U;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V))\subsetneq L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U,V)
and the inclusion is strict. See for example
[
HvNVW16
, Remark 1.2.16]
.

(v)

In the definition
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{s}(U,V)
means there are extensions
\tilde{f}_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
and
\tilde{f}_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
such that
\tilde{f}_{1}|_{U\times V}=\tilde{f}_{2}|_{U\times V}=f
. When
U
and
V
are smooth domains, by Lemma
2.8
below we can choose a suitable extension
\tilde{f}_{1}=\tilde{f}_{2}
by taking
\tilde{f}=E_{x}E_{s}f=E_{s}E_{x}f
that extends
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}
and
L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
simultaneously.

(vi)

Let
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\beta_{0},\beta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. Clearly
\min(2^{j\alpha_{0}+k\beta_{0}},2^{j\alpha_{1}+k\beta_{1}})\leq 2^{j((1-\theta)\alpha_{0}+\theta\alpha_{1})+k((1-\theta)\beta_{0}+\theta\beta_{1})}
for
\theta\in[0,1]
. Thus we have inclusion: for
U,V
either the total space or bounded smooth convex domains,

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{0}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{0}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{1}}(U,V)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{\theta}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{\theta}}(U,V),\quad\alpha_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)\alpha_{0}+\theta\alpha_{1},\ \beta_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)\beta_{0}+\theta\beta_{1}.

(2.3)

Lemma 2.8

(Common extensions)
.

Let
R>0
, and let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two bounded domain with smooth boundaries.
Then there are extension operators
E_{x}:L^{\infty}(U)\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
E_{s}:L^{\infty}(V)\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
such that

(a)

E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
E_{s}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
are both bounded linear for all
-R<\alpha<R
.

(b)

E_{x}E_{s},E_{s}E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
are well-defined and equal.

Moreover
\tilde{E}:=E_{x}E_{s}
is an extension operator for
U\times V
that has boundedness

\tilde{E}:\mathscr{X}\mathscr{Y}(U,V)\to\mathscr{X}\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q}),\quad\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}:-R<\alpha<R\}.

(2.4)

Proof.

The construction
E_{x}
and
E_{s}
for
(a)
can be found in
[
Tri10
, Theorems 2.9.2, 2.9.4 (i) and 3.3.4 (i)]
, which are both
L^{\infty}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-bounded for
-R<\alpha<R
.

For continuous functions
u\in C^{0}(U)
,
v\in C^{0}(V)
we have equality
E_{x}E_{s}(u\otimes v)=E_{x}u\otimes E_{s}v=E_{s}E_{x}(u\otimes v)
. Thus by taking finite linear combinations of simple tensors and taking
C^{0}
-limit, we see that
E_{x}E_{s}=E_{s}E_{x}:C^{0}(U\times V)\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
is an extension operator for
U\times V
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
(\phi_{j}),(\psi_{k})
be dyadic resolutions for
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
,
\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
respectively. Clearly for
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{-R+\varepsilon}\mathscr{C}^{-R+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, the double sum
\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast\tilde{f}
converges to
\tilde{f}
in
\mathscr{C}^{-R+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\mathscr{C}^{-R+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
. Since
((\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast\tilde{f})|_{U\times V}\in C^{0}(U\times V)
for each
j,k
, by Definition
2.6

(iii)
we see that
E_{x}E_{s}f=E_{s}E_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{-R+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\mathscr{C}^{-R+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
whenever
f\in\mathscr{C}^{-R+\varepsilon}\mathscr{C}^{-R+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
. Therefore
E_{x}E_{s},E_{s}E_{x}\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
are both defined and equal. This proves
(b)
.

Note that the same argument also shows that

\phi_{j}\ast_{x}(E_{s}g)(x,s)=E_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}g)(x,s),\quad\text{for }(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q},\quad j\geq 0,\quad g\in\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}(U,\mathbb{R}^{q}).

(2.5)

To prove (
2.4
), we only prove the case
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}
for
-R<\alpha<R
. The argument for the rest of the cases are similar and we omit the details.

Using the boundedness
E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
E_{s}:L^{\infty}(V)\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{E}(\tilde{f}|_{U\times V})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}=\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}(E_{s}E_{x}(\tilde{f}|_{U\times V}))\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}=\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|E_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}E_{x}(\tilde{f}|_{U\times V}))\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|E_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}}\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{s\in V}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}E_{x}(\tilde{f}(\cdot,s)|_{U})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|E_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|E_{x}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\|\tilde{f}(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}\lesssim_{\tilde{E}}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}.

For a function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V)
, taking the infimum of all extensions
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
over
f
, we get
\|\tilde{E}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V)}
from the above inequality.
∎

More generally, for a linear operator
T=T_{x}
acting on functions in
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
, we have natural extension
\tilde{T}=T\otimes\operatorname{id}_{s}
acting on functions in
U\times V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
R_{1}<R_{2}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be either the total spaces or bounded smooth domains. Suppose
T
is a linear operator on functions of
U
such that
T:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+r}(U)
for all
R_{1}<\alpha<R_{2}
. Then
(\tilde{T}f)(x,s):=T(f(\cdot,s))(x)
defines a linear operator that has boundedness
\tilde{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(U,V)
for all
R_{1}<\alpha<R_{2}
and
\mathscr{X}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\beta}:\beta\in\mathbb{R}\}
.

Proof.

By passing to an extension
\tilde{E}=E_{x}E_{s}
given in Lemma
2.8
, it suffices to consider the case
U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V=\mathbb{R}^{q}
. We only prove the case
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
since the case
\mathscr{X}=L^{\infty}
is similar but simpler.

Clearly for
u\in\mathscr{C}^{R_{1}+}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
v\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
we have
\tilde{T}(u\otimes v)=Tu\otimes v
and
\psi_{k}\ast_{s}\tilde{T}(u\otimes v)=Tu\otimes(\psi_{k}\ast v)
for all
k\geq 0
. Taking finite linear combinations of simple tensors and then taking limit similar to the proof of Lemma
2.8

(b)
, we get
\tilde{T}:\mathscr{C}^{R_{1}+\varepsilon}\mathscr{C}^{-R}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{R_{1}+r+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\mathscr{C}^{-R-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
is defined and bounded for all
R>0
and
0<\varepsilon<R_{2}-R_{1}
, and we have similar to (
2.5
) that

\psi_{k}\ast_{s}(\tilde{T}f)=\tilde{T}(\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f),\quad k\geq 0,\quad f\in\mathscr{C}^{R_{1}+}\mathscr{C}^{-R}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q}).

Therefore, for
R_{1}<\alpha<R_{2}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{T}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+r}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}=\sup_{j,k\geq 0}2^{j(\alpha+r)+k\beta}\|(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast\tilde{T}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}=\sup_{j,k\geq 0}2^{j(\alpha+r)+k\beta}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}\tilde{T}(\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\tilde{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+r}}\sup_{j,k\geq 0}2^{j(\alpha+r)+k\beta}\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\|\phi_{j}\ast((\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f)(\cdot,s))\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\tilde{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+r}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}.

This completes the proof.
∎

We define the mixed Hölder-Zygmund space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
as the following

Definition 2.10
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
, and let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two convex open sets. We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}(U,V)
to be the set of all continuous functions
f:U\times V\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}(U,V)}:=\sup_{x\in U}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)}+\sup_{s\in V}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}<\infty.

We use abbreviation
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
when the domain is clear. We use
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
when the variables are also clear.

Remark 2.11
.

(i)

The norm definition is symmetric and we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\alpha}_{s,x}
. But in applications we mostly refer the left variable as space variable, and the right variable as parameter. For the notation
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
, we usually use
\alpha\geq\beta
.

(ii)

When
\alpha=\beta
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. See Lemma
2.12

(ii)
below. So our
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces is a generalization to the single variable cases.

(iii)

We can define three variables mixed Zygmund spaces and the version on manifold analogously. See Definition
2.15

(iv)
and Definition
3.2
.

Also see Definition
2.15
when
\alpha,\beta
are not real numbers.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
, and let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two bounded convex open sets with smooth boundaries.

(i)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
with equivalent norms.

(ii)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)
with equivalent norms.

Proof.

(i)
: By Lemma
2.8
we have an extension operator
\tilde{E}:=E_{x}E_{s}=E_{s}E_{x}:L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, thus

\textstyle\sup_{x\in U}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)}\geq\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{x\in U}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)}=\|E_{s}f\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,\mathbb{R}^{q})}\approx\|\tilde{E}f\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\approx\|f\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)}.

(2.6)

Similarly
\sup_{s\in V}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}\gtrsim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V)}
. Therefore
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)}\gtrsim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)}
.

For the converse direction, it suffices to show the first inequality in (
2.6
) is indeed an equality.
By Remark
2.7

(vi)
we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{0}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\beta}{2}}(U,V)\subset C^{0}(U,V)
. Thus
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
can be pointwise defined, and the essential supremum is indeed the supremum. This completes the proof.

(ii)
: Clearly
\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V)}\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)}
and
\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)}
for each
x\in U
and
s\in V
. Therefore
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}(U,V)}\leq 2\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)}
.

For the converse, we can choose
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
and
(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
as dyadic resolutions for
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
respectively, such that
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{0}\subset B^{n}(0,\sqrt{2})
and
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{0}\subset B^{q}(0,\sqrt{2})
additionally. Define
(\pi_{l})_{l=0}^{\infty}
as
\pi_{0}=\phi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}
and
\pi_{l}=2^{l(n+q)}\pi_{0}(2^{l}\cdot)-2^{(l-1)(n+q)}\pi_{0}(2^{l-1}\cdot)
for
l\geq 1
. We see that
(\pi_{l})_{l}
is a dyadic resolutions for
\mathbb{R}^{n+q}
and

\textstyle\pi_{l}=\sum_{\max(j,k)=l}\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k}=\phi_{l}\otimes\delta_{0\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}+\delta_{0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\otimes\psi_{l}-\sum_{\min(j,k)=l}\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k},\quad\forall\ l\geq 0.

Therefore for
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n+q})
, by Lemma
2.2

(i)
and Remark
2.7

(ii)
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\pi_{l}\ast\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\phi_{l}\ast_{x}\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\psi_{l}\ast_{s}\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j=l}^{\infty}\|\psi_{l}\ast_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}\tilde{f})\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{k=l+1}^{\infty}\|\phi_{l}\ast_{x}(\psi_{k}\ast_{s}\tilde{f})\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{\alpha}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}}2^{-l\alpha}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}}2^{-l\alpha}+\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}}\sum_{j=l}^{\infty}2^{-j\alpha}+\|\phi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}}\sum_{k=l+1}^{\infty}2^{-k\alpha}\lesssim\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}}2^{-l\alpha}.

Thus
\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}
. Taking restrictions to the domain
U\times V
we finish the proof.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
and let
U,V
be two bounded smooth domains. Since
\mathscr{C}^{0}\subset L^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{C}^{1}
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
.

Remark 2.14

(Comparison of different working spaces)
.

The bi-parameter structures used in
[
Gon20
, Section 2]
and
[
NW63
, Section 4.4]
are the
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces, which are similar to our
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces. We recall them here:

Let
k\geq l\geq 0
be integers, let
r,s\in[0,1)
and let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two open (bounded smooth) subsets. The space
\mathcal{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)
is the set of all continuous functions
f:U\times V\to\mathbb{R}
whose norm below is finite:

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)}:=\sum_{j=0}^{l}\sum_{i=0}^{k-j}\big(\sup_{s\in V}\|\nabla_{x}^{i}\nabla_{s}^{j}f(\cdot,s)\|_{C^{0,r}(U)}+\sup_{x\in U}\|\nabla_{x}^{i}\nabla_{s}^{j}f(x,\cdot)\|_{C^{0,s}(V)}\big).

Here
C^{0,\gamma}
is the standard Hölder space
(0\leq\gamma<1)
.

One can see that
\mathcal{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)
when
r,s\neq 0
and
\mathcal{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)
when
r
or
s=0
. We leave the proof to readers.

The
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces is better than the
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces in some sense. On one hand
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
are only defined for
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor\geq\lfloor\beta\rfloor
, while
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
are defined for all positive
\alpha,\beta
. On the other hand, the discussions of
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
are always separated between the integer cases and non-integer cases, while for
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces the discussion can be unified as we have complex interpolations,

[\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(U,V),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}(U,V)]_{\theta}=\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha_{0}+\theta\alpha_{1},(1-\theta)\beta_{0}+\theta\beta_{1}}(U,V),\quad\text{for all }\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\beta_{0},\beta_{1}>0,\quad\theta\in[0,1].

(2.7)

We also leave the proof of (
2.7
) to readers.

Here are more definitions.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
and let
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two open convex sets. We define the following spaces:

(i)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V):=\{f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\times V):f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U^{\prime},V^{\prime}),\ \forall U^{\prime}\Subset U,\ V^{\prime}\Subset V\}
.

(ii)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta-}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta-,\alpha}(U,V):=\bigcap_{0<\gamma<\beta}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\gamma}(U,V)
.

(iii)

\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}(U,V):=\bigcap_{\gamma>0}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\beta}(U,V)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V):=\bigcap_{\gamma>0}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
.

(iv)

Let
\gamma>0
and let
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be as well an open convex set. We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(U,V,W)
to be the set of all continuous functions
f:U\times V\times W\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}_{x,s,t}(U,V,W)}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in U,s\in V}\|f(x,s,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(W)}+\sup_{x\in U,t\in W}\|f(x,\cdot,t)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)}+\sup\limits_{s\in V,t\in W}\|f(\cdot,s,t)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}<\infty.

Remark 2.16
.

By Lemma
2.12

(i)
we can say that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}_{x,s,t}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s,t}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}L^{\infty}_{x,t}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s,t}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}_{s,t}
, where
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s,t}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}L^{\infty}_{x,t}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x,s}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s,t}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}_{s,t}
are defined analogously by Definitions
2.6
and
2.10
.

For
0<\alpha\leq\infty
,
0<\beta<\infty
, that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}
does not imply
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
.

Example 2.17
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
. We consider the following
f:\mathbb{R}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}
:

f(x,s):=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}2^{-l(\alpha+1)}\exp(i2^{l}x+i2^{\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}l}s).\quad\text{ We have }\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(x,s)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}i2^{-l\alpha}\exp(i2^{l}x+i2^{\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}l}s).

One can check that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}
and
f\in L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
. So
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}
.

Let
\psi=(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution. By (
2.1
)
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{k}=\operatorname{supp}2^{k-1}\hat{\psi}_{1}\subsetneq(-2^{k+1},-2^{k-1})\cup(2^{k-1},2^{k+1})
for
k\geq 1
, so there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{k}\subseteq 2^{k}\cdot\big([-2+\varepsilon_{0},-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}]\cup[\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{0},2-\varepsilon_{0}]\big)
. Note that there are infinitely many integer pairs
(k,l)
such that
|k-\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}l|<\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}
, and for such a pair
(\phi_{k}\ast_{s}f)(x,s)=i2^{-l\alpha}\exp(i2^{l}x+i2^{\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}l}s)
.

So
\|\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\approx 2^{-k\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}}
holds for infinite many
k\geq 0
, which means
f\notin L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}+\varepsilon}_{s}
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

In general, for
0<\alpha,\beta<\infty
and a function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}
, the best we can guarantee is
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}}_{x,s}
, where the index
\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}
is optimal.
For applications we need the following endpoint optimal result.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
and let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two bounded convex smooth domains. Then
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}-}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded.
In particular, if
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}(U,V)
, then
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta-}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

Clearly
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
is bounded. By Remark
2.7

(vi)
we have embedding
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\subset\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha-\theta}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\theta\beta}_{s}
for all
\theta\in(0,1)
. Thus
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\subset\bigcap_{0<\theta<\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha-\theta}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\theta\beta}_{s}\subset L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}-}_{s}
. This proves the boundedness
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}-}_{x,s}
.

Let
\alpha\to+\infty
we get
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}_{x,s}\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta-}_{x,s}
.
∎

On the other hand, if
f(x,s)
is, say a harmonic function in
x
, then the assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}
can imply
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
. In fact we have
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
by the following.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\beta>0
, and let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two bounded open sets with smooth boundaries. Suppose
f\in\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
satisfies
\Delta_{x}f=0
. Then
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
. In particular
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V)
and
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\times V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

As a corollary, let
\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}
be an open set. If
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\tilde{U}\times V;\mathbb{C})
is holomorphic in
z
, then
\partial_{z}g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\tilde{U}\times V;\mathbb{C}^{m})
holds.

Proof.

Since the result is local in
x\in U
, it is enough to consider the case where
U=\mathbb{B}^{n}
is the unit ball.

Let
E_{s}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
be an
s
-variable extension as in Lemma
2.8
. Clearly
\nabla_{x}^{k}E_{s}f\in\mathscr{C}^{-k-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{2^{k}})
for every
k\geq 0
.

Let
(\psi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution for
\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
. By Lemma
B.1

(iv)
we have

2^{j\beta}|\psi_{j}\ast_{s}E_{s}\nabla_{x}^{k}f(x,s)|\lesssim_{n,k}(1-|x|)^{-k-1}2^{j\beta}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{s}E_{s}\nabla_{x}^{k}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-k-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta,E}\|\nabla_{x}^{k}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-k-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},V)}.

Taking supremum in
s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
j\geq 0
, by Lemmas
2.2

(i)
we get

\sup_{|\nu|\leq k}\|\partial_{x}^{\nu}f\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(r\mathbb{B}^{n},V)}\lesssim_{V,n,k,\beta}(1-r)^{-k-1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},V)},\quad\forall 0<r<1.

Therefore
f\in\mathscr{C}^{k}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},V)
for all
k
, which means
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},V)
. The results
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
follow immediately.

When
g(z,s)
is holomorphic in
z
, we know
g
is complex harmonic in
z
-variable. So we reduce it to the real case by considering
\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2m}
.
∎

2.2
A proposition for bi-parameter products

In this subsection we are going to compute the mixed regularity of products using bi-parameter paraproducts.

Recall in Definition
2.6
, we fix two dyadic resolutions
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to define the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}
-norms and the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-norms.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
and
\eta,\theta\in[0,1]
satisfy
\eta+\theta<\alpha
. There is a
C=C_{\alpha,\beta,\eta,\theta,\phi,\psi}>0
such that

\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})},

for all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

We postpone the proof to the end of this subsection.

For applications, we use the cases
(\eta,\theta)=(1,0)
and
(\eta,\theta)=(0,0)
to prove Proposition
4.3
that the left hand side of (
4.3
) behave well. And we use
(\eta,\theta)=(0,1)
to prove Proposition
2.27
which is required in Proposition
4.3

(iii)
.

Corollary 2.21
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
and
\eta,\theta\in[0,1]
satisfy
\eta+\theta<\alpha
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two bounded open convex subsets with smooth boundaries. Then the product operation is bilinear bounded as the following:

[(f,g)\to fg]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V).

(2.8)

In particular it is bilinearly bounded between the following spaces: for
\alpha>1
,

(i)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
.

(ii)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
.

(iii)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
.

Proof.

Indeed
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
are the cases where
(\eta,\theta)=(0,1)
,
(\eta,\theta)=(0,0)
and
(\eta,\theta)=(1,0)
in (
2.8
) respectively.

By Lemma
2.8
there is an extension operator such that
\tilde{E}:\mathscr{X}(U,V)\to\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
and
\tilde{E}:\mathscr{Y}(U,V)\to\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
. Here we use
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and
\mathscr{Y}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
.
Applying Proposition
2.20
,

\|fg\|_{\mathscr{Y}(U,V)}\leq\|\tilde{E}f\tilde{E}g\|_{\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\lesssim\|\tilde{E}f\|_{\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\|\tilde{E}g\|_{\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}(U,V)}\|g\|_{\mathscr{Y}(U,V)}.

So we obtain (
2.8
).
∎

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
and
\eta\in[0,1]
satisfy
\eta<\alpha
. Let
m,n,q\geq 0
and let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}
be bounded convex open sets with smooth boundaries.
Then there is a
c_{0}=c_{0}(U,V,m,n,q,\alpha,\beta,\eta)>0
, such that for any matrix-valued function
A:U\times V\to\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
satisfying
\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}<c_{0}
, we have:

•

A(x,s)\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
is an invertible matrix for every
(x,s)\in U\times V
.

•

\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}
.

Moreover, the map of inverting matrices

[A\mapsto A^{-1}]:\{A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}):\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}<c_{0}\}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}),

is real-analytic and in particular Fréchet differentiable.

Proof.

Applying Corollary
2.21
to the components of the matrix product
(A-I_{m})^{k}=(A-I_{m})^{k-1}(A-I_{m})
recursively in
k
, with the parameter
\theta=0
, we know there is a
C=C(U,V,m,n,q,\alpha,\beta,\eta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|(A-I_{m})^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq C^{k-1}\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}^{k},\quad\forall\ k\geq 1,\ A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}).

Now take
c_{0}=\frac{1}{2}C^{-1}
, we see that the sum
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(I_{m}-A)^{k}
is norm convergent in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
whose limit equals to
(I_{m}-(I_{m}-A))^{-1}=A^{-1}
. So
A(x,s)
is an invertible matrix for all
(x,s)\in U\times V
.

That
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
is closed under products also implies that
[A\mapsto A^{-1}=I+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(I-A)^{k}]
is a real-analytic map in the open set
\{A:\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}<c_{0}\}
with respect to the norm-topology.
∎

Remark 2.23
.

By taking
\eta=0
and forgetting the
s
-variable, we get the following classical result:

For any
\alpha>0
and bounded convex smooth open
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there is a
c_{0}^{\prime}(U,m,\alpha)>0
, such that

\forall A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}),\quad\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}<c_{0}^{\prime}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}.

The proof of Proposition
2.20
use paraproduct decompositions. The single parameter case is the following:

Lemma 2.24

(The paraproduct decomposition)
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a dyadic resolution. For bounded continuous functions
u,v
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, consider the following decomposition

uv=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)+\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)+\sum_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v).

(2.9)

Then for every
l\geq 0
,

\phi_{l}\ast(uv)=\phi_{l}\ast\bigg(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)+\sum_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)\bigg).

(2.10)

Remark 2.25
.

In
[
BCD11
, Chapter 2.8.1, (2.41)]
(
2.9
) is denoted by
uv=T(u,v)+T(v,u)+R(u,v)
where
T
is the paraproduct operator associated with
\phi
.

Proof.

(
2.10
) can be obtained by investigating the Fourier support condition in Definition
2.1
. Note that
\phi_{l}\ast\left((\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)\right)\neq 0
only when
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{l}\cap(\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j}+\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j^{\prime}})\neq\varnothing
, and by (
2.1
) we have on the following:

\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{l}\subset\begin{cases}\{2^{l-1}<|\xi|<2^{l+1}\},&l\geq 1,\\
\{|\xi|<2\},&l=0;\end{cases}\quad\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j}+\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j^{\prime}}\subset\begin{cases}\{2^{\max(j,j^{\prime})-2}<|\xi|<2^{\max(j,j^{\prime})+2}\},&|j-j^{\prime}|\geq 3,\\
\{|\xi|<2^{\max(j,j^{\prime})+2}\},&|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2.\end{cases}

For details, see
[
Tri10
, Proof of Theorem 2.8.2]
,
[
Gra14
, Chapter 4.4]
or
[
BCD11
, Proof of Theorem 2.52]
. Note that their choice of decompositions (
2.9
) may have slight differences.
∎

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded convex open set with smooth boundary.

(i)

There is a
C=C(U,\alpha,\beta)\geq 0
such that
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U)}
for all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
,
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U)
.

(ii)

There is a
C=C(U,\alpha)\geq 0
such that
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}\leq C(\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)})
for all
f,g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
.

Proof.

See
[
Tri10
, Theorem 3.3.2(ii)]
for
(i)
and
[
BCD11
, Corollary 2.86]
for
(ii)
. Note that by Lemma
2.2
and Definition
2.4
Hölder-Zygmund spaces are a special case to Besov spaces
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}(U)
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

We now prove Proposition
2.20
.

Proof of Proposition
2.20
.

We fix
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
as two dyadic resolutions for their spaces.
Note that by assumption
f,g
are both
L^{\infty}
-functions, so their product is already defined in
L^{\infty}(U\times V)
. What we need is to show that
fg
lays in the desired function class.

Since
\alpha-\theta>0
, by Lemma
2.26

(i)
for almost every
s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\|f(\cdot,s)g(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha-\theta}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|g(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}}
,
with the implied constant being independent of
f
and
g
. So by taking essential supremum of
s
, we get

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}=

\displaystyle\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\|f(\cdot,s)g(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}

(2.11)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}.

Our goal is to show the bilinear control
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
.

Before we move into the computations, we first upgrade (
2.10
) to the bi-parameter case.

For bounded functions
f
and
g
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we denote

f_{jk}:=(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast f,\quad g_{jk}:=(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast g,\quad(fg)_{jk}:=(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast(fg),\quad j,k\geq 0.

We can write
\displaystyle fg=\bigg(\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}+3\leq j}+\sum\limits_{j+3\leq j^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}\bigg)\bigg(\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}+3\leq k}+\sum\limits_{k+3\leq k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}\bigg)(f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}})
as
3\times 3
sums:

\displaystyle fg=\sum_{\mu,\nu=1}^{3}S^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}

(2.12)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{j=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{j=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\sum\limits_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}\sum\limits_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}\sum\limits_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}.

Here
S^{\mu\nu}
,
1\leq\mu,\nu\leq 3
, is the
\mu
-th row
\nu
-th column term in (
2.12
). We denote

S^{\mu\nu}_{lq}:=\|(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast S^{\mu\nu}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})},\quad l,q\geq 0.

Applying Lemma
2.24
on
x
-variable and
s
-variable separately we get

(fg)_{lq}=(\phi_{l}\otimes\psi_{q})\ast\Bigg(\sum\limits_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\\
j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\end{subarray}}\Bigg)\Bigg(\sum\limits_{k=q-2}^{q+2}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}+\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=q-2}^{q+2}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2\\
k,k^{\prime}\geq q-3\end{subarray}}\Bigg)(f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}).

(2.13)

The assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{0}
implies that
|f_{jk}|\lesssim_{\phi,\psi}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{0}}2^{-j\alpha}
, and the assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
implies that
|f_{jk}|\lesssim_{\phi,\psi}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}2^{j\eta-k\beta}
. Similar control works for
g
. To conclude this, we have

\displaystyle|f_{jk}|\lesssim_{\phi,\psi}a_{jk}\cdot\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}},\quad|g_{jk}|\lesssim_{\phi,\psi}b_{jk}\cdot\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}},

\displaystyle\text{where }a_{jk}:=\begin{cases}2^{-j\alpha},&j\geq\frac{\beta k}{\alpha+\eta}\\
2^{j\eta-k\beta},&j\leq\frac{\beta k}{\alpha+\eta},\end{cases}\quad b_{jk}:=\begin{cases}2^{-j(\alpha-\theta)},&j\geq\frac{\beta k}{\alpha+\eta}\\
2^{j(\eta+\theta)-k\beta},&j\leq\frac{\beta k}{\alpha+\eta},\end{cases}\quad\text{for }j,k\geq 0.

(2.14)

For convenience we can normalize
f
and
g
so that
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}=\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}=1
.

Note that when
l\geq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}
, by (
2.11
) we have

|(fg)_{lq}|\lesssim 2^{-l(\alpha-\theta)}\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{0}}\lesssim 2^{-l(\alpha-\theta)}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}=2^{-l(\alpha-\theta)}\leq 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}.

So we only need to show that

\textstyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}|(fg)_{lq}(x,s)|\leq\sum_{\mu,\nu=1}^{3}S^{\mu\nu}_{lq}\lesssim_{\phi,\psi,\alpha,\beta,\theta,\eta}2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta},\qquad\text{for }l\leq\tfrac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}.

(2.15)

We prove the control by estimating
S^{\mu\nu}_{lq}
one by one. We use the following facts for geometric sums,

\sum_{j=u}^{v}2^{j\gamma}\leq\begin{cases}C_{\gamma}2^{u\gamma},&\gamma<0,\\
v-u,&\gamma=0,\\
C_{\gamma}2^{v\gamma},&\gamma>0,\end{cases}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\sum_{j=0}^{v}2^{j\gamma}\leq C_{\gamma}\min(v2^{v\gamma},1),\quad\text{for }u,v\geq 0.

(2.16)

For the first term
S^{11}_{lq}
, by (
2.14
) we have
