S^{11}_{lq}\leq\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\sum_{k=q-2}^{q+2}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}a_{jk}b_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}
. By (
2.16
) we know
\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{k=q-2}^{q+2}a_{jk}\leq C_{\alpha,\eta,\theta}\cdot a_{lq}
, so we only need to consider the sums for
j^{\prime},k^{\prime}
and omit
j,k
, as the following

\displaystyle\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\sum_{k=q-2}^{q+2}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}a_{jk}b_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\lesssim a_{lq}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{l}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{q}b_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\leq 2^{l\eta-q\beta}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{l}\Big(\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}j^{\prime}}2^{-j^{\prime}(\alpha-\theta)}+\sum_{\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}j^{\prime}\leq k^{\prime}\leq q}2^{j^{\prime}(\eta+\theta)-k^{\prime}\beta}\Big)

(2.17)

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{l\eta-q\beta}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{l}(j^{\prime}2^{-j^{\prime}(\alpha-\theta)}+2^{j^{\prime}(\eta+\theta)-j^{\prime}(\alpha+\eta)})\lesssim 2^{l\eta-q\beta}\leq 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}.

The deductions for the rest of the terms are similar, we sketch them as below. Note that the assumption
\alpha-\eta-\theta>0
implies
\frac{2\alpha-\theta}{\alpha+\eta}>1
:

\displaystyle S_{lq}^{12}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{j\leq l}\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq q}a_{jq}b_{lk^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{j\leq l}2^{j\eta-q\beta}\Big(\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}l}2^{-l(\alpha-\theta)}+\sum_{\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}l\leq k^{\prime}\leq q}2^{l(\eta+\theta)-k^{\prime}\beta}\Big)

(2.18)

\displaystyle\lesssim l2^{l\eta-q\beta}(l2^{-l(\alpha-\theta)}+2^{l(\eta+\theta)-l(\alpha+\eta)})=2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}(l^{2}2^{-l\alpha}+l2^{-l\alpha})\lesssim 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}.

\displaystyle S_{lq}^{13}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq l}\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq q}a_{l^{\prime}q}b_{l^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{l^{\prime}\leq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{l^{\prime}\eta-q\beta}\Big(\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}l^{\prime}}2^{-l^{\prime}(\alpha-\theta)}+\sum_{\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}l^{\prime}\leq k^{\prime}\leq q}2^{l^{\prime}(\eta+\theta)-k^{\prime}\beta}\Big)+\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{-l^{\prime}\alpha}\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq q}2^{-l^{\prime}(\alpha-\theta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l^{\prime}\leq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{l^{\prime}\eta-q\beta}(l^{\prime}2^{-l^{\prime}(\alpha-\theta)}+2^{-l^{\prime}(\alpha-\theta)})+\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{-l^{\prime}(2\alpha-\theta)}\lesssim 2^{-q\beta}\sum_{l^{\prime}\leq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}l^{\prime}2^{-l^{\prime}(\alpha-\eta-\theta)}+2^{-\frac{2\alpha-\theta}{\alpha+\eta}\beta q}\lesssim 2^{-q\beta}.

\displaystyle S_{lq}^{21}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}\leq l}\sum_{k\leq q}a_{lk}b_{j^{\prime}q}\leq\Big(\sum_{k\leq\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}l}2^{-l\alpha}+\sum_{\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}l\leq k\leq q}2^{l\eta-k\beta}\Big)\sum_{j^{\prime}\leq l}2^{j^{\prime}(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}\lesssim(l2^{-l\alpha}+2^{-l\alpha})\cdot l2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}\lesssim 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}.

\displaystyle S_{lq}^{22}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{j\leq l}\sum_{k\leq q}a_{jk}b_{lq}\leq\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}a_{jk}\cdot 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{j\geq\frac{\beta k}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{-j\alpha}+\sum_{j\leq\frac{\beta k}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{j\eta-k\beta}\Big)2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}\lesssim 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}.

\displaystyle S_{lq}^{23}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq l}\sum_{k\leq q}a_{l^{\prime}k}b_{l^{\prime}q}\leq\sum_{l^{\prime}\leq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}\Big(\sum_{k\leq\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}l^{\prime}}2^{-l^{\prime}\alpha}+\sum_{\frac{\alpha+\eta}{\beta}l^{\prime}\leq k\leq q}2^{l^{\prime}\eta-k\beta}\Big)2^{l^{\prime}(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}+\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}\sum_{k\leq q}2^{-l^{\prime}\alpha}2^{-l^{\prime}(\alpha-\theta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l^{\prime}\leq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}(l^{\prime}2^{-l^{\prime}\alpha}+2^{-l^{\prime}\alpha})\cdot 2^{l^{\prime}(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}+\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{-l^{\prime}(2\alpha-\theta)}\lesssim 2^{-q\beta}\sum_{l^{\prime}\leq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}l^{\prime}2^{-l^{\prime}(\alpha-\eta-\theta)}+2^{-\frac{2\alpha-\theta}{\alpha+\eta}\beta q}\lesssim 2^{-q\beta}.

\displaystyle S_{lq}^{31}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}\leq l}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}a_{lq^{\prime}}b_{j^{\prime}q^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{j^{\prime}\leq l}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}2^{l\eta-q^{\prime}\beta}2^{j^{\prime}(\eta+\theta)-q^{\prime}\beta}\lesssim l2^{l(2\eta+\theta)}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}2^{-2q^{\prime}\beta}\lesssim 2^{l(\eta+\theta)}2^{-q\beta}(l2^{l\eta-q\beta})\overset{l\leq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}}{\lesssim}2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}.

\displaystyle S_{lq}^{32}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{j\leq l}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}a_{jq^{\prime}}b_{lq^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{j\leq l}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}2^{j\eta-q^{\prime}\beta}2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q^{\prime}\beta}\lesssim l2^{l(2\eta+\theta)}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}2^{-2q^{\prime}\beta}\lesssim 2^{l(\eta+\theta)}2^{-q\beta}(l2^{l\eta-q\beta})\lesssim 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}.

\displaystyle S_{lq}^{33}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq l}\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}a_{l^{\prime}q^{\prime}}b_{l^{\prime}q^{\prime}}\leq\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}\Big(\sum_{l\leq l^{\prime}\leq\frac{\beta q^{\prime}}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{l^{\prime}\eta-q^{\prime}\beta}2^{l^{\prime}(\eta+\theta)-q^{\prime}\beta}+\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq\frac{\beta q^{\prime}}{\alpha+\eta}}2^{-l^{\prime}\alpha}2^{-l^{\prime}(\alpha-\theta)}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}(q^{\prime}2^{q^{\prime}(\frac{2\eta+\theta}{\alpha+\eta}\beta-2\beta)}+2^{-\frac{2\alpha-\theta}{\alpha+\eta}\beta q^{\prime}})=\sum_{q^{\prime}\geq q}(q^{\prime}2^{-\frac{2\alpha-\theta}{\alpha+\eta}\beta q^{\prime}}+2^{-\frac{2\alpha-\theta}{\alpha+\eta}\beta q^{\prime}})\lesssim q2^{-\frac{2\alpha-\theta}{\alpha+\eta}\beta q}\lesssim 2^{-q\beta}\leq 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}.

We conclude that
S^{\mu\nu}_{lq}\lesssim 2^{l(\eta+\theta)-q\beta}
for all non-negatives
l\leq\frac{\beta q}{\alpha+\eta}
and all
1\leq\mu,\nu\leq 3
. Thus (
2.15
) holds and we have finished the proof.
∎

2.3
Compositions and inverse mapping with parameter

In this subsection we work on the concept of compositions between parameter dependent maps. We are going to prove an auxiliary result, Proposition
2.27
, that is used in Proposition
4.3

(iii)
. We recall that for a bijection
f:U_{1}\to U_{2}
, we denote its inverse map by
f^{\mathrm{Inv}}:U_{2}\to U_{1}
.

In this subsection, for maps
F(x,s):U\times V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, usually we would denote by
\tilde{F}
the map
\tilde{F}(x,s):=(F(x,s),s)
. Note that if
F(\cdot,s)
has inverse for every
s
, then in this notation we have

\tilde{F}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y,s)=(F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y),s).

Let
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
and
y=(y^{1},\dots,y^{n})
be two standard coordinate systems of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
be the standard coordinate system of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
. We let
\mathcal{I}(x,s):=x
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

Proposition 2.27
.

Let
\alpha>1
and
\beta\in(0,\alpha+1]
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta=\delta(n,q,\alpha,\beta,\varepsilon)>0
such that the following is true:

Suppose
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
satisfy

(F-\mathcal{I})|_{(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}\equiv 0\quad\text{and}\quad\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}<\delta.

(2.19)

Then
\tilde{F}(x,s):=(F(x,s),s)
is bijective on
\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
.
Moreover, by setting
\tilde{\Phi}(y,s):=\tilde{F}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y,s)
for
(y,s)\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
, we have
g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
and

\|g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}<\varepsilon.

(2.20)

We postpone the proof to the end of this subsection.

Remark 2.28
.

In Proposition
2.27
we may have
g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
.
But in applications, the Proposition
4.7
, our result in Proposition
2.27
is enough.

The proof is a combination of quantitative controls for composition and inverse mapping. Recall the quantitative results for the one-parameter cases:

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
m,n\geq 1
be integers. Let
\alpha>0
.

(i)

Let
M>0
and
\beta>0
, such that
\max(\alpha,\beta)>1
. There is a
K_{1}=K_{1}(m,n,\alpha,\beta,M)>0
satisfying the following:

Suppose
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
satisfies
g(\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{m}
and
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})}\leq M
. Then for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})
, we have
f\circ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
with
\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq K_{1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}
.

(ii)

There is a
K_{2}(n,\alpha)>0
satisfying the following:

Suppose
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfy
\|F-\operatorname{id}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{2}^{-1}
and
F|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv\operatorname{id}
. Then
F
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, and for the inverse map
F^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}
we have
\|F^{\mathrm{Inv}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{2}
.

Proof.

For the proof of
(ii)
, see
[
SY21
, Proposition 4.10]
.

For
(i)
, see
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.8]
for the case
\alpha>1
. We prove the case
0<\alpha\leq 1<\beta
below.

By Lemma
2.2

(iii)
we have
\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\approx_{n,\alpha}\|f\circ g\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sup\limits_{x_{0}\neq x_{1}}|\tfrac{f(g(x_{0}))+f(g(x_{1}))}{2}-f(g(\tfrac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2}))|\cdot|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{-\alpha}
and
|\tfrac{f(g(x_{0}))+f(g(x_{1}))}{2}-f(\tfrac{g(x_{0})+g(x_{1})}{2})|\lesssim_{m,\beta}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}}|g(x_{0})-g(x_{1})|^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}
for
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Therefore

\displaystyle|\tfrac{f(g(x_{0}))+f(g(x_{1}))}{2}-f(g(\tfrac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2}))|\leq|\tfrac{f(g(x_{0}))+f(g(x_{1}))}{2}-f(\tfrac{g(x_{0})+g(x_{1})}{2})|+|f(\tfrac{g(x_{0})+g(x_{1})}{2})-f(g(\tfrac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2}))|

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{m,\beta}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}}|g(x_{0})-g(x_{1})|^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}+\|f\|_{C^{0,1}}|\tfrac{g(x_{0})+g(x_{1})}{2}-g(\tfrac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2})|

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{n,\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha/\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}}^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{\alpha}+\|f\|_{C^{0,1}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{\beta}\lesssim_{M}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{\alpha}.

In particular we can choose
K_{1}(m,n,\alpha,\beta,M)\approx_{m,n,\alpha,\beta}(1+M)^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}
.
∎

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
n,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in(0,\alpha+1]
. Write
\mathcal{I}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
\mathcal{I}(x,s):=x
. There is a
K_{3}=K_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>1
that satisfies the following:

Let
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies
\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{3}^{-1}
and
(F-\mathcal{I})|_{(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}\equiv 0
. Then

(i)

For each
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
,
F(\cdot,s):\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}
is bijective.

(ii)

Define
\Phi(y,s):=F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y)
for
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
,
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
. Then
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and satisfies

\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq K_{3}.

(2.21)

Proof.

We let
K_{3}
be a large constant which may change from line to line.

Let
K_{2}=K_{2}(n,\alpha)>0
be the constant in Lemma
2.29

(ii)
. So we can choose
C_{1}>0
such that

\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{1}^{-1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\|F(\cdot,s)-\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{2}^{-1},\ \forall s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}.

By assumption
(F(\cdot,s)-\mathcal{I}(\cdot,s))|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=(F(\cdot,s)-\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv 0
, so by Lemma
2.29

(ii)

F(\cdot,s):\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism for each
s\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and we have

\textstyle\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{1}^{-1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{2}.

(2.22)

In particular
\Phi(y,s)
is pointwise defined for
(y,s)\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
. This finishes the proof of
(i)
.

Let
c^{\prime}_{0}=c^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n},n,\alpha)\in(0,1)
be the constant in Remark
2.23
, we have, for every
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
,

\|\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s)-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<c^{\prime}_{0}\ \Rightarrow\ \|(\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s))^{-1}-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<2c^{\prime}_{0}.

(2.23)

Thus we can find a
C_{2}\geq\max(2c^{\prime}_{0}{}^{-1},C_{1},K_{2},1)
, such that

\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{2}^{-1}\ \Rightarrow\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}|(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|\leq\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|(\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s))^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<C_{2}.

(2.24)

In particular
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}|\det(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|>C_{2}^{-n}
. Thus when
0<\beta<\min(\frac{2\alpha+1}{\alpha+1},\frac{3}{2})
(in particular when
0<\beta\leq\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}
), we get
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}1
by applying Proposition
A.1
.

It remains to prove the case
\beta>\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}
. By chain rule, we have

0_{q\times n}=\nabla_{s}(F(\Phi(y,s),s))=\big((\nabla_{x}F)(\Phi(y,s),s)\big)\cdot\nabla_{s}\Phi(y,s)+(\nabla_{s}F)(\Phi(y,s),s).

Therefore

\nabla_{s}\Phi(y,s)=-((\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{s}F)\circ(\Phi(y,s),s).

(2.25)

What we need is to show
\|\nabla_{s}\Phi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{q\times n})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}1
. We will proceed by induction on
k=\lceil\beta-\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}\rceil
. The base case
k=1
has done since we have obtained
K_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
for
\beta\leq\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}
from the above discussion.

Suppose the case
k\geq 1
is done. Let
\beta
satisfies
\lceil\beta-\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}\rceil=k+1
. We denote
\gamma(\beta):=\max(\beta-1,\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1})>1
. By induction hypothesis and
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma(\beta)}_{(x,s)}}\lesssim\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\gamma(\beta)}_{x,s}}
there is a
C_{3}=C_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
such that

\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\gamma(\beta)}}\leq K_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\gamma(\beta))^{-1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma(\beta)}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{3}.

(2.26)

Applying Lemma
2.29

(i)
to (
2.25
), taking
f
to be the components of
(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{s}F\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{q\times n})
, we have, when the assumption of (
2.19
) is satisfied,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{s}\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{x,s}\|((\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{s}F)(\Phi(y,\cdot),s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}+\sup_{x,s}\|((\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{s}F)(\Phi(y,s),\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}

(2.27)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K_{1}(n,q,\gamma(\beta),\beta-1,C_{3})\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\nabla_{s}F(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle+K_{1}(q,q,\gamma(\beta),\beta-1,\|\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma(\beta)}})\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\nabla_{s}F(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}.

Similar to the argument of (
2.24
), by considering the constants
c^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n},n,\beta-1)
and
c^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{B}^{q},n,\beta-1)
in Remark
2.23
, we can find a
C_{4}=C_{4}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>\max(C_{2},C_{3},K_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\gamma(\beta)))
, such that

\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{4}^{-1}\ \Rightarrow\ \sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|(\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s))^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|(\nabla_{x}F(x,\cdot))^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<C_{4}.

Thus
\|(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\nabla_{s}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{q\times n})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}1
. Combining this with (
2.27
) we see that
\sup\limits_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|\Phi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}1
. Thus we get a
K_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)
for such
\beta
, finishing the induction and hence the whole proof.
∎

To prove Proposition
2.27
we also need an auxiliary result for Laplacian.

Lemma 2.31
.

Let
\alpha>-1
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two bounded convex open sets with smooth boundaries. Then the
x
-variable Laplacian
\Delta_{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x^{j}}^{2}
has a bounded linear inverse
\mathcal{P}
as the following map

\Delta_{x}:\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}\mathscr{X}_{s}(U,V):f|_{(\partial U)\times V}=0\}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{x}\mathscr{X}_{s}(U,V),\quad\mathscr{X}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\beta}:\beta\in\mathbb{R}\}.

In particular
\mathcal{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{X}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\mathscr{X}(U,V)
is bounded linear.

Proof.

Indeed, for any
v\in\mathscr{C}^{(-2)+}(U)
there is a unique continuous
u\in C^{0}(\overline{U})
satisfying
\Delta u=v
and
u\big|_{\partial U}=0
. Moreover the solution operator
[v\mapsto u]:\mathscr{C}^{\mu-1}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\mu+1}(U)
is bounded linear for all
\mu>-1
. See
[
FR95
, Theorem 15]
. One can also see
[
GT01
, Theorems 6.19 and 8.33]
for the cases where
\mu
is positive non-integer.

We can denote the above solution operator as
\mathcal{P}_{0}
. So
\mathcal{P}_{0}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(U)
is bounded linear. Therefore by Lemma
2.9
,
\mathcal{P}f(x,s):=\mathcal{P}_{0}(f(\cdot,s))(x)
gives the bounded operator
\mathcal{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{X}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\mathscr{X}(U,V)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.27
.

We have
\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
by Remark
2.13
. Therefore there is a
\delta_{1}>0
such that if (
2.19
) is satisfied with
\delta=\delta_{1}
, then
\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq K_{3}^{-1}
, wh ere
K_{3}=K_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
is the constant in Lemma
2.30
. Thus by Lemma
2.30

(i)

\Phi(y,s)=F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y)
is well-defined and
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}}\leq K_{3}
holds.

Applying Lemma
2.29

(i)
along with its constant
K_{1}
, we have for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|f\circ(\Phi(\cdot,s),s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle\leq K_{1}(n,n,\alpha+1,\alpha+1,K_{3})\cdot\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})};

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|f\circ(\Phi(y,\cdot),s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle\leq K_{1}(n,q,\alpha+1,\beta,K_{3})\cdot\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})};

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|f\circ(\Phi(y,s),\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle\leq K_{1}(q,q,\alpha+1,\beta,\|\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}})\cdot\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

Therefore we can find a
C_{1}=C_{1}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
such that

\|f\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}\leq C_{1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})},\quad\forall f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q}).

(2.28)

In particular by taking
f
to be the components of
F-\mathcal{I}
, we have
\|\mathcal{I}-\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
. By
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
in Remark
2.13
and that
\nabla_{y}\mathcal{I}(y,s)=I_{n}
, we know
\|\nabla_{y}\Phi-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{y}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{y}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}}\lesssim\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}}
. Thus, we can find a
C_{2}=C_{2}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
such that

\|\nabla_{y}\Phi-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq C_{2}\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})},\text{ when }\eqref{Eqn::Hold::CompThm::Assum}\text{ is satisfied with }\delta=\delta_{1}.

By Corollary
2.22
(with
\eta=1
), we see that if
C_{2}\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
is small enough, then
(\nabla_{y}\Phi)^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Therefore we can find a
\delta_{2}\in(0,\delta_{1})
such that

\|(\nabla_{y}\Phi)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\delta_{2}^{-1},\text{ when }\eqref{Eqn::Hold::CompThm::Assum}\text{ is satisfied with }\delta=\delta_{2}.

(2.29)

Now for
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
, we take
G_{j}=\partial_{x^{j}}\mathcal{P}g
where
\mathcal{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
is the operator in Lemma
2.31
. Thus
G=(G_{1},\dots,G_{n})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

g\circ\tilde{\Phi}=(\operatorname{div}_{x}G)\circ\tilde{\Phi},\qquad(\nabla_{x}G)\circ\tilde{\Phi}=\nabla_{y}(G\circ\tilde{\Phi})\cdot(\nabla_{y}\Phi)^{-1}.

By Corollary
2.21

(iii)
we have

\displaystyle\|g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{y}(G\circ\tilde{\Phi})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\|(\nabla_{y}\Phi)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}.

Therefore by (
2.29
), (
2.28
) and Remark
2.13
, we have: when (
2.19
) is satisfied with
\delta=\delta_{2}
,

\displaystyle\|g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|G\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|G\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|G\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|G\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}.

In other words, there is a
C_{3}=C_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
, such that

\|g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}\leq C_{3}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})},\quad\forall\ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q}).

Now for every
\varepsilon>0
, take
\delta=\min(C_{3}^{-1}\varepsilon,\delta_{2})
, we get (
2.20
) and complete the proof.
∎

In applications, we also need a local version of the composition estimate.

Lemma 2.32
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta>0
. Let
U_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
,
V_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}_{t}
,
U_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{y}
and
V_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{s}
be four open sets.

(i)

Suppose
\max(\alpha,\gamma)>1
and
\max(\beta,\gamma)>1
. Let
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};U_{2})
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{2},V_{2})
. We define
h:U_{1}\times V_{1}\times V_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
as
h(x,t,s):=f(g(x,t),s)
. Then
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\gamma),\min(\beta,\gamma),\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1},V_{2})
.

(ii)

Let
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
be a matrix function such that
A(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is invertible for every
(x,s)
. Then
A^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
as well.

(iii)

Let
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(x_{0},t_{0})\in U_{1}\times V_{1}
. Suppose
\nabla_{x}F(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is an invertible matrix. Then there are neighborhoods
U_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{1}
of
x_{0}
,
V_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq V_{1}
of
t_{0}
and
\Omega_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
F(x_{0})
such that

•

For each
t\in V^{\prime}_{1}
,
F(\cdot,t):U^{\prime}_{1}\to F(U^{\prime}_{1},t)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is bijective and
F(U^{\prime}_{1},t)\supseteq\Omega_{1}
.

•

Set
\Phi(x,t):=F(\cdot,t)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(x)
, then
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1},V^{\prime}_{1};U^{\prime}_{1})
.

In particular for the map
\tilde{F}(x,t):=(F(x,t),t))
we have that
\tilde{F}:U_{1}^{\prime}\times V_{1}^{\prime}\to\tilde{F}(U^{\prime}_{1}\times V^{\prime}_{1})
is homeomorphism.

Proof.

By passing to local and taking scaling, we can assume
U_{1},V_{1},U_{2},V_{2}
are all unit balls, and every maps have finite norms.

For
(i)
, what we need to show is that
\sup_{t\in V_{1},s\in V_{2}}\|h(\cdot,t,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\gamma)}(U_{1})}
,
\sup_{x\in U_{1},s\in V_{2}}\|h(x,\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma)}(V_{1})}
and
\sup_{x\in U_{1},t\in V_{1}}\|h(x,y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\delta}(V_{2})}
are all finite.

Clearly
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\delta}_{s}L^{\infty}_{x,t}
because
\sup_{x,t}\|h(x,t,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\delta}(V_{2})}\leq\sup_{y\in U_{2}}\|f(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\delta}(V_{2})}
.

By Lemma
2.29

(i)
(since we assume
V_{1}
and
U_{2}
are balls) we have a
C>0
that depends on
U_{2},V_{1},\beta,\gamma
and the upper bound of
\sup_{s^{\prime}\in V_{2}}\|g(\cdot,s^{\prime})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U_{1})}
but not on
t\in V_{1}
or
s\in V_{2}
, such that

\|h(\cdot,t,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\gamma)}(U_{1})}\leq C\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U_{2})}.

Taking supremum over
t\in V_{1}
and
s\in V_{2}
we get
\sup_{t\in V_{1},s\in V_{2}}\|h(\cdot,t,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\gamma)}(U_{1})}<\infty
.

Switching the role of
x
and
t
we get
\sup\limits_{x\in U_{1},s\in V_{2}}\|h(x,\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma)}(V_{2})}<\infty
, finishing the proof of
(i)
.

For
(ii)
, by assumption
\det A:U_{1}\times V_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
is non-vanishing in the domain. By continuity and by shrinking
U_{1},V_{1}
we can assume that
\inf_{U\times V}|\det A|>c
for some
c>0
. By
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.7]
(where we take
X=\nabla
in the reference), we get
\sup_{x}\|\det A(x,\cdot)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}}<\infty
and
\sup_{s}\|\det A(\cdot,s)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}}<\infty
. So
(\det A)^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
.

By Cramer’s rule, the entries of
A^{-1}
are the products of
(\det A)^{-1}
and the entries of
A
, thus
A^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
.

For
(iii)
, apply the Inverse Function Theorem on
F(\cdot,t_{0})
, we can find a neighborhood
\tilde{U}_{1}\subseteq U_{1}
of
x_{0}
such that
F(\cdot,t_{0}):\tilde{U}_{1}\to F(\tilde{U}_{1},t_{0})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism. Shrinking
\tilde{U}_{1}
if necessary, we can assume
F(\cdot,t_{0}),F(\cdot,t_{0})^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\tilde{U}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
both have bounded
C^{1}
-norm.

By (
2.3
) we have
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\mathscr{C}^{0}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2(\alpha+1)}}_{s}
, thus
[t\mapsto F(\cdot,t)]:V_{1}\to C^{1}(\tilde{U}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a continuous map (in fact Hölder) with respect to the
C^{1}
-norm topology. Since the set of bounded
C^{1}
-embedding map in
C^{1}(\tilde{U}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is open, we can now find a neighborhood
\tilde{V}_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
of
t_{0}
such that
F(\cdot,t)
is
C^{1}
-embedding for all
t\in\tilde{V}_{1}
. By Inverse Function Theorem again, the inverse map of
F(\cdot,t):\tilde{U}_{1}\to F(\tilde{U}_{1},t)
is also
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
.

Take
U^{\prime}_{1}:=\tilde{U}_{1}
and take
\Omega_{1}
such that
F(x_{0},t_{0})\in\Omega_{1}\Subset F(U^{\prime}_{1},t_{0})
. By continuity there is a neighborhood
V^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq\tilde{V}_{1}
of
t_{0}
such that
\Omega_{1}\Subset F(U^{\prime}_{1},t)
for all
t\in V^{\prime}_{1}
as well. Now we get
U^{\prime}_{1},V^{\prime}_{1},\Omega_{1}
as desired and
\Phi:\Omega_{1}\times V^{\prime}_{1}\to U^{\prime}_{1}
is pointwise defined.

Since
F(\cdot,t)
is
C^{1}
-embedding which is homeomorphic to its image, the result
\sup_{t\in V^{\prime}_{1}}\|\Phi(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\Omega_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
then follows from
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.9]
.

When
\beta\leq 1
, the result
\sup_{x\in\Omega_{1}}\|\Phi(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
follows from Proposition
A.1
via a scaling. When
\beta>1
, applying Inverse Function Theorem to the
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}
-map
[(x,t)\in U^{\prime}_{1}\times V^{\prime}_{1}\mapsto(F(x,t),t)]
, we see that its inverse
[(x,t)\in\Omega_{1}\times V^{\prime}_{1}\mapsto(\Phi(x,t),t)]
is also
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}
, which means
\sup_{x\in\Omega_{1}}\|\Phi(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}(V_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
.

Therefore either case we have
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{t}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}_{t}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}_{x,t}
.

Now
\tilde{F}
and
\tilde{F}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}\subset C^{0}
so
\tilde{F}
is homeomorphic to its image.
∎

3
The Vector Fields and Ordinary Differential Equations

3.1
Review of tangent subbundles and involutivity

In this part we give some conventions of notations and terminologies.

For
\kappa>1
, we can define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-maps between two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds, since we have compositions
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Notation 3.1
.

Let
\kappa>1
,
\alpha\in(0,\kappa]
, and let
\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N}
be two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-differential manifolds.
We use
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathfrak{N})
as the set of all (locally)
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-maps from
\mathfrak{M}
to
\mathfrak{N}
.

We can define the mixed regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
on product manifolds.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\kappa>1
and
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\kappa]
. Let
\mathfrak{M}^{m},\mathfrak{N}^{n},\mathfrak{P}^{q}
be three
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds.

We say a continuous map
f:\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathfrak{P}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, denoted by
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N};\mathfrak{P})
, if for every coordinate charts
\phi:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\psi:V\subseteq\mathfrak{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\rho:W\subseteq\mathfrak{P}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
that satisfy
f(U\times V)\subseteq W
, we have

\rho\circ f\circ(\phi^{\mathrm{Inv}},\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\phi(U),\psi(V);\mathbb{R}^{q}).

(3.1)

Remark 3.3
.

(i)

The definition still has symmetry for indices:
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N};\mathfrak{P})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{N},\mathfrak{M};\mathfrak{P})
.

(ii)

To check
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
it suffices to show (
3.1
) is valid on a coordinate cover
\{(\phi_{i},\psi_{i},\rho_{i})\}_{i\in I}
for
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\times f(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\times\mathfrak{P}
such that
f(U_{i}\times V_{i})\subseteq W_{i}
for each
i\in I
. The proof can be done using Lemma
2.32

(i)
. Such cover always exists because of the continuity of
f
. We leave the proof to readers.

(iii)

In applications we only consider differentiable maps localized near a fixed point. By passing to a precompact coordinate chart near a point, everything is reduced to the case of Euclidean space, and by shrinking the domains every functions or vector fields would have bounded Hölder-Zygmund regularities.

Definition
3.2
is applied for labeling component-wise regularity for vector fields and parameterizations with parameters.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
1<\alpha\leq\kappa
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
m
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold. We say
\Phi:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathfrak{M}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-regular parameterization
, if
\Phi
is injective and
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\Phi(\Omega)\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-coordinate chart.

When
\kappa>2
, the (real) tangent bundle
T\mathfrak{M}
of a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
has a natural
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}
-differential structure. Its complexification, the complex tangent bundle
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}:=T\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathbb{C}=T\mathfrak{M}\oplus iT\mathfrak{M}
is also a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}
-differential manifold. So we can define (real or complex)
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields when
\alpha\in(0,\kappa-1]
.

Similarly, the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
and the complex cotangent bundle
\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
are also
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}
-manifolds.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\kappa>2
,
0<\alpha\leq\kappa
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold. A
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex tangent subbundle of rank
r
is a subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
satisfying that for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
,

•

\mathcal{S}_{p}:=\mathcal{S}\cap\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
is a
r
-dimensional complex vector subspace.

•

There is an open neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
on
U
, such that
X_{1}|_{q},\dots,X_{r}|_{q}
form a complex linear basis for
\mathcal{S}_{q}
for all
q\in U
.

We denote it as
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
. And we say
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
form a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis
for
\mathcal{V}
(or
\mathcal{S}
) on
U
.

In the paper we do not deal with abstract vector bundle, we will use “subbundle” as the abbreviation of (complex) tangent subbundle.

The real tangent subbundles can be defined in the same way through replacing every
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
by
T\mathfrak{M}
.
But we focus mostly on complex subbundles since every real subbundle
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
can be identified as a complex subbundle
\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathbb{C}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
.

Alternatively a rank
r

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
can be equivalently defined as a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-section of the complex Grassmannian bundle
\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{C}}(r,\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})=\coprod_{p\in\mathfrak{M}}\{\text{rank }r\text{ complex spaces of }\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}\}
. For their equivalence we leave the proof to readers.

In Theorem
5.1
we consider tangent subbundles with mixed regularities.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
0<\alpha,\beta\leq\kappa
and let
\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N}
be two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds. A
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-complex tangent subbundle of rank
r
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}
-subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N})
such that, for any
(u_{0},v_{0})\in\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
there is a neighborhood
U\times V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
of
(u_{0},v_{0})
and complex vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}T(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}))
, such that
X_{1}|_{(u,v)},\dots,X_{r}|_{(u,v)}
form a complex linear basis for
\mathcal{S}_{(u,v)}
for all
(u,v)\in U\times V
.

Equivalently this is saying
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N};\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{C}}(r,\mathbb{C}T(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N})))
as a section of the Grassmannian bundle.

Recall the notion of involutive structure in Definition
1.5
. Let
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
be a local coordinate system, for (at least
C^{1}
) vector fields
X=:\sum_{j=1}^{n}X^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
and
Y=:\sum_{k=1}^{n}Y^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
, we recall that

[X,Y]=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\Big(X^{j}\frac{\partial Y^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}-Y^{j}\frac{\partial X^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}.

(3.2)

For completeness we give definition of the involutivity on subbundles with mixed regularities.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\kappa>2
,
\alpha\in(1,\kappa]
and
\beta\in(0,\kappa]
. Let
\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N}
be two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds and
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-complex subbundle such that
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
. We say
\mathcal{S}
is involutive, if for every
C^{0}
-sections
X,Y:\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{S}
that are
C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
in
\mathfrak{M}
, the commutator
[X,Y]
is a
C^{0}
-section of
\mathcal{S}
.

Remark 3.8
.

Definition
3.7
does not require
X
or
Y
to be differentiable on
\mathfrak{N}
, since when
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
the differentiations in (
3.2
) are only taken on
\mathfrak{M}
but not on
\mathfrak{N}
. This is critical for the case
\beta\leq 1
.

An important fact for involutive structures is that we can pick a collection of good basis.

Lemma 3.9

(Canonical local basis)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
,
\kappa\geq\max(\alpha,\beta)+1
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
be two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-complex subbundle over
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
such that
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
.

Let
p\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\lambda_{0}\in\mathfrak{N}
. Then there are numbers
r,m,q\geq 0
and a (mixed real and complex)
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-coordinate system
(t,z,s)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m},s^{1},\dots,s^{q}):\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
near
p
, such that

\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}\oplus\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{\bar{z}^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\partial_{\bar{z}^{m}}|_{p},\partial_{s^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\partial_{s^{q}}|_{p})=\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}

(3.3)

Moreover there are a neighborhood
U\subseteq\tilde{U}
of
p
, a neighborhood
V\subseteq\mathfrak{N}
of
\lambda_{0}
, and a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}
-local basis
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{S}
on
U\times V
, such that the collection vectors
X^{\prime}=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r}]^{\top}
and
X^{\prime\prime}=[X_{r+1},\dots,X_{r+m}]^{\top}
are of the form

\begin{pmatrix}X^{\prime}\\
X^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}&&A^{\prime}&B^{\prime}\\
&I_{m}&A^{\prime\prime}&B^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial/\partial t\\
\partial/\partial z\\
\partial/\partial{\bar{z}}\\
\partial/\partial s\end{pmatrix},

(3.4)

where
A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{r\times m})
,
A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
,
B^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{r\times q})
and
B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times q})
. In addition,

(i)

Such
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}
are uniquely determined by the coordinate system
(t,z,s)
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{S}
is involutive, then
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}
are pairwise commutative.

(iii)

Suppose
\mathcal{S}=\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, then we must have
m=0
,
A^{\prime}=0
,
A^{\prime\prime}=0
,
B^{\prime\prime}=0
and
B^{\prime}
is real-valued.

Proof.

Clearly
\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}
and
\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}
are both complex linear subspaces of
\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. We have
r:=\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{(p,\lambda_{0})})
,
m:=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}-r
and
q:=\dim\mathfrak{M}-\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{(p,\lambda_{0})})
. Note that
\dim\mathfrak{M}=r+2m+q
. See
[
BCH08
, Lemma I.8.5]
for details of (
3.3
).

The formula (
3.4
) follows from the standard linear algebra argument. See
[
Lun92
, Lemma 1]
or
[
BCH08
, Page 18]
. Note that by Lemma
2.32

(ii)
, if
M\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times(r+m)})
is a matrix function invertible at every point in the domain, then
M^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times(r+m)})
holds as well. We leave the details to readers.

(i)
: If
\tilde{X}=[\tilde{X}_{1},\dots,\tilde{X}_{r+m}]^{\top}
is another local basis that has expression (
3.4
), then
\tilde{X}-X
are linear combinations of
\partial_{\bar{z}}
and
\partial_{s}
. But
\mathcal{S}|_{U}\cap\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{\bar{z}},\partial_{s})=\{0\}
, so
\tilde{X}=X
is the unique collection.

(ii)
: We use
(\partial_{u^{1}},\dots,\partial_{u^{r+m}}):=(\partial_{t^{1}},\dots,\partial_{t^{r}},\partial_{z^{1}},\dots,\partial_{z^{m}})
,
(\partial_{v^{1}},\dots,\partial_{v^{m+q}}):=(\partial_{\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\partial_{\bar{z}^{m}},\partial_{s^{1}},\dots,\partial_{s^{q}})
and
\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&B^{\prime}\\
A^{\prime\prime}&B^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\left(f_{j}^{k}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq r+m\\
1\leq k\leq m+q\end{subarray}}
for simplicity.

To show that
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}
are commutative, by direct computation, for
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq r+m
,

[X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}]=\sum_{k=1}^{r+m}\bigg(\frac{\partial f_{j^{\prime}}^{k}}{\partial u^{j}}-\frac{\partial f_{j}^{k}}{\partial u^{j^{\prime}}}+\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{m+q}\Big(f_{j}^{k^{\prime}}\frac{\partial f_{j^{\prime}}^{k}}{\partial v^{k^{\prime}}}-f_{j^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}}\frac{\partial f_{j}^{k}}{\partial v^{k^{\prime}}}\Big)\bigg)\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{k}}\in\operatorname{Span}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}},\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Big).

(3.5)

But by involutivity
[X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}]
are all sections of
\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Span}X
. Since
\operatorname{Span}X\cap\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{\bar{z}},\partial_{s})=\{0\}
by (
3.4
), we get
[X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}]=0
for
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq r+m
.

(iii)
: When
\mathcal{S}=\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, the complex conjugate
\bar{X}_{1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}
also form a local basis for
\mathcal{S}
. By
(i)
we see that
\bar{X}^{\prime}=X^{\prime}
, so
B^{\prime}=\bar{B}^{\prime}
is a real-valued matrix.

By (
3.4
) we have
X^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{z}+A^{\prime\prime}\partial_{\bar{z}}+B^{\prime\prime}\partial_{s}
and
\bar{X}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{\bar{z}}+\bar{A}^{\prime\prime}\partial_{z}+\bar{B}^{\prime\prime}\partial_{s}
. They are linearly independent since
\partial_{z}
and
\partial_{\bar{z}}
are linearly independent. So
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m},\bar{X}_{r+1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}
are all sections
\mathcal{S}
and are also linearly independent, which means
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}\geq r+2m
. Since
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
we must have
m=0
, which means
A^{\prime}=0
,
A^{\prime\prime}=0
,
B^{\prime\prime}=0
and
B^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{r\times q})
.
∎

We recall the definition of complex Frobenius structure.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\alpha>1
and let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex subbundle. We call
\mathcal{S}
a
complex Frobenius structure
of
\mathfrak{M}
, if
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a subbundle, and both
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
are involutive.

Remark 3.11
.

In
[
Gon20
]

\mathcal{S}
is also called a
Levi-flat CR vector bundle
on
\mathfrak{M}
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a complex tangent subbundle, we use
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
as the
dual bundle
of
\mathcal{S}
, and
\bar{\mathcal{S}}
as the
complex conjugate
of
\mathcal{S}
. They are given by

\mathcal{S}^{\bot}:=\{(p,\theta)\in\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}:\theta(v)=0,\forall v\in\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}\}\leq\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M},\quad\bar{\mathcal{S}}:=\{(p,\bar{v})\in\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}:(p,v)\in\mathcal{S}\}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}.

(3.6)

Remark 3.13
.

(i)

One can check that
\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is indeed a subbundle, since
\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\leq\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
is a complex subspace (which is closed under the scalar multiplication by
i
) for each
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

(ii)

The fact
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is a subbundle does not necessary implies
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
or
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a subbundle. For example, the
Mizohata operator

X=\partial_{t}+it\partial_{x}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{t,x}
generates a rank 1 complex subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathbb{R}^{2}
, but
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
do not have constant rank:

(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})_{(t,x)}=\begin{cases}0,&t\neq 0,\\
\mathbb{C}_{t}\times\{0\},&t=0,\end{cases}\quad(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})_{(t,x)}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}_{t,x}^{2},&t\neq 0,\\
\mathbb{C}_{t}\times\{0\},&t=0.\end{cases}

(iii)

Suppose
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a subbundle. Then it is possible that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is more regular than
\mathcal{S}
. For example, in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with coordinates
(x,y,z)
,
\mathcal{S}
is spanned by
\partial_{x}+i(1+|y|)\partial_{y}
.
\mathcal{S}
is merely a Lipschitz subbundle, but
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
can be spanned by
\partial_{x},\partial_{y}
. So
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a analytic subbundle. Similarly if
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a subbundle than it can be more regular than
\mathcal{S}
as well. In this case
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}=\{0\}
is a rank 0 subbundle which formally speaking is real-analytic as well.

In the definition of complex Frobenius structure, we have no regularity assumption on
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. But the following lemma shows that once
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
has constant rank then
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
are both subbundles with at least
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-regularities.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\alpha>1
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold. Let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex subbundle such that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
has constant rank at every point, then
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex tangent subbundles.

Proof.

Fix a point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
. By Lemma
3.9

\mathcal{S}
has a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m})
that has the form (
3.4
).

At the point
p
, by (
3.4
) we see that
X_{1}|_{p},\dots,X_{r+m}|_{p},\bar{X}_{r+1}|_{p},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}|_{p}
are linearly independent and span
\mathcal{S}_{p}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{p}=(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})_{p}
. By assumption
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
has constant rank, so
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r+2m
. Clearly by (
3.4
)
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m},\bar{X}_{r+1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}
are still linear independent near
p
. Therefore
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle since
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m},\bar{X}_{r+1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}
form a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis.

Note that
[\bar{X}_{1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r},X_{r+1},\dots,X_{r+m},\bar{X}_{r+1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}]^{\top}
also have the form (
3.4
) and form a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis for
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. By Lemma
3.9

(i)
we have
X_{1}=\bar{X}_{1},\dots,X_{r}=\bar{X}_{r}
, which means
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are linear independent sections of
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

Now
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})=2\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}-\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r
we see that
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
form a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis for
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. In particular
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is also a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle.
∎

If
\mathfrak{M}
is a complex manifold and
\mathcal{S}
is a holomorphic subbundle, we have a similar result to Lemma
3.9
.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
m
-dimensional complex manifold. A holomorphic vector field on
\mathfrak{M}
is a complex vector field such that locally it has form
\sum_{k=1}^{m}f^{k}(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}
where
(z^{1},\dots,z^{m}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
is a complex (holomorphic) coordinate chart for
\mathfrak{M}
, and
f^{k}
are holomorphic functions on
U
.

A rank
r
holomorphic tangent subbundle over
\mathfrak{M}
is a complex tangent subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and holomorphic vector fields
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{r}
on
U
that that form a local basis for
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
.

Equivalently
\mathcal{S}
is a holomorphic section of the Grassmannian bundle
\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{C}}(r,T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M})\subset\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{C}}(r,\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
, where
T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}})
is the holomorphic tangent bundle.

In the proof of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, after applying Frobenius theorem on
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, we need to show that the pullback complex Frobenius structure
\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}
has the same regularity as
\mathcal{S}
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
is a
(r+q)
-dimensional complex manifold and
\mathcal{S}\leq T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}
be a rank
r
holomorphic subbundle. Then for any point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a holomorphic chart
(z,w)=(z^{1},\dots,z^{r},w^{1},\dots,w^{q}):\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{p}\oplus\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}|_{p})=T^{\mathcal{O}}_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. Here
T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}
is the holomorphic tangent bundle.

Moreover for such chart
(z,w)
there is a neighborhood
U
of
p
, and a unique holomorphic local basis
Z=[Z_{1},\dots,Z_{r}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{S}
on
U
that has the form
Z=\partial_{z}+A\partial_{w}
where
A\in\mathcal{O}(U;\mathbb{C}^{r\times n})
.

Proof.

The existence of
(z,w)
follows the same but simpler argument from the existence of the chart
(t,z,s)
in Lemma
3.9
.

Fix
(z,w)
, we can find a holomorphic local basis
W=[W_{1},\dots,W_{r}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{S}
near
p
that has expression
W=M^{\prime}\partial_{z}+M^{\prime\prime}\partial_{w}
for some holomorphic matrix functions
M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}
defined near
p
. Since
\mathcal{S}_{p}\oplus\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}|_{p})=T^{\mathcal{O}}_{p}\mathfrak{M}
,
M^{\prime}(p)\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
is invertible. By continuity
M^{\prime}
is invertible in some neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
. And by cofactor representation of a holomorphic matrix we know
M^{\prime-1}
is also a holomorphic matrix function on
U
.

Take
Z=M^{\prime-1}W=\partial_{z}+M^{\prime-1}M^{\prime\prime}\partial_{w}
in
U
, so
A:=M^{\prime-1}M^{\prime\prime}
is the desired holomorphic matrix function.

Since
\mathcal{S}
is a complex tangent subbundle of
\mathfrak{M}
and we
\mathcal{S}_{p}\oplus\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{\bar{z}}|_{p},\partial_{\operatorname{Re}w}|_{p},\partial_{\operatorname{Im}w}|_{p})=\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. By Lemma
3.9

(i)
,
A
is uniquely determined by the mixed real and complex coordinate system
(z,\operatorname{Re}w,\operatorname{Im}w)
, thus determined by
(z,w)
.
∎

Finally we give an auxiliary result to illustrate the regularity of the pullback subbundle.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\kappa>2
,
\alpha>0
and
\beta\geq\alpha
such that
1<\beta\leq\kappa-1
. Let
0\leq r\leq m\leq n
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
manifold and
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex tangent subbundle of rank
r
.

Suppose
\Phi:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\to\mathfrak{M}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-regular parameterization such that
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\Omega);T\mathfrak{M})
and
\mathcal{S}|_{\Phi(\Omega)}\leq\operatorname{Span}(\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}})
(here
\operatorname{Span}
is for the complex span). Then
\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\Omega
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle as well.

In general, if
\beta<\alpha+1
, it is possible that
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m+1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}
are only
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\supset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha})
.

Proof.

Let
u_{0}\in\Omega
, we need to show that
\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}
has a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis near
u_{0}
.

By assumption
\mathcal{S}
has
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
near
\Phi(u_{0})\in\mathfrak{M}
. Thus
(\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{r})
is a local basis for
\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}
near
u_{0}
.

Since
\mathcal{S}|_{\Phi(\Omega)}\leq\operatorname{Span}(\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}})
, we can write
X_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}f_{j}^{k}\cdot\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
for
1\leq j\leq r
, for some functions
f_{j}^{k}
defined near
\Phi(u_{0})
. Since
f_{j}^{k}
are uniquely determined by
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
and
\Phi
, we see that
f_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

Taking pullback by
\Phi
, we have
\Phi^{*}X_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}(f_{j}^{k}\circ\Phi)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
, since
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, we get
f_{j}^{k}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, so
(\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{r})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis for
\mathcal{S}
, finishing the proof.
∎

3.2
Review of autonomous ODE flows

In the real Frobenius theorem and the holomorphic Frobenius theorem we need ODE flows to construct the parameterizations.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
X
be
C^{1}
-vector field on a
C^{2}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
. The
flow map
of
X
, denoted by
\exp_{X}:\mathcal{D}_{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\times\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}
, is defined in the way that for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
,
t\mapsto\exp_{X}(t,p)
is the unique maximal solution
\gamma=\gamma_{p}
to the ODE

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\dot{\gamma}(t)=X(\gamma(t))\in T_{\gamma(t)}\mathfrak{M},\\
\gamma(0)=p.\end{array}\right.

Here
\mathcal{D}_{X}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{p\})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the maximal existence interval of
\gamma
, which is an open interval containing
0
.

We denote
e^{tX}(p):=\exp_{X}(t,p)
.

Remark 3.19
.

There is no ambiguity to use
e^{tX}
since
(t,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
if and only if
(1,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{tX}
and
\exp_{X}(t,p)=\exp_{tX}(1,p)
for all
(t,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
.

From classical ODE theories, we know
\exp_{X}
is well-defined, and
\mathcal{D}_{X}
is an open set in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathfrak{M}
. Moreover
\mathcal{D}_{X}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathfrak{M}
is the whole space when either
\mathfrak{M}
is a compact manifold or
X
has compact support. For such cases
e^{tX}
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism of
\mathfrak{M}
for every
t
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
X
and
Y
be
C^{1}
-vector fields defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with compact supports. Then

(e^{tX})^{*}Y=Y+\int_{0}^{t}(e^{uX})^{*}[X,Y]du,\quad\text{on }\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\forall\ t\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.7)

In particular, if
[X,Y]=0
holds on some open subset
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then for any
U_{1}\Subset U_{0}
there is a
\varepsilon>0
such that
e^{tX}e^{sY}=e^{sY}e^{tX}=e^{tX+sY}
holds in
U_{1}
for
|t|,|s|<\varepsilon
.

Here for a diffeomorphism
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the pullback vector field
F^{*}X:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be written as
(X\cdot\nabla F^{\mathrm{Inv}})\circ F
, where we use row vectors
X=(X^{1},\dots,X^{n})
and
(X\cdot\nabla G)^{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}X^{k}\frac{\partial G^{j}}{\partial x^{k}}
for
j=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

The standard Lie derivative formula shows that
\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}((e^{hX})^{*}Y-Y)=[X,Y]
as a continuous vector field. By
(e^{tX})^{*}X=X
, we have
\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}((e^{(t+h)X})^{*}Y-(e^{tX})^{*}Y)=[X,(e^{tX})^{*}Y]=(e^{tX})^{*}[X,Y]
pointwisely. Taking the definite integral from
0
to
t
we get (
3.7
).

Now assume
[X,Y]=0
in
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Take
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})\max(\|X\|_{C^{0}(U_{0};\mathbb{R}^{n})},\|Y\|_{C^{0}(U_{0};\mathbb{R}^{n})})^{-1}
, so
e^{tX}
,
e^{sY}
and
e^{sY}e^{tX}e^{-sY}
are all defined in
U_{1}
for
|t|,|s|<\varepsilon
.

Using (
3.7
) with
[X,Y]=0
we see that
(e^{tX})_{*}Y=(e^{-tX})^{*}Y=Y
. Therefore for
|t|,|s|<\varepsilon_{0}
, in
U_{1}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}(e^{sY}\circ e^{tX}\circ e^{-sY})=(Y-(e^{tX})_{*}Y)\circ e^{sY}e^{tX}e^{-sY}=0.

Thus
e^{sY}\circ e^{tX}\circ e^{-sY}=e^{tX}
as continuous maps.

On the other hand for
u\in[0,1]
, on the domain we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial u}(e^{utX}e^{usY})=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}(e^{utX})e^{usY}+\frac{\partial}{\partial u}(e^{usY})e^{utX}=(tX+sY)\circ e^{utX}e^{usY}
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial u}e^{u(tX+sY)}=(tX+sY)\circ e^{u(tX+sY)}
. So
u\mapsto e^{utX}e^{usY}
and
u\mapsto e^{u(tX+sY)}
are both ODE flows of
tX+sY
, which are equal by the flow uniqueness. Taking
u=1
we get
e^{tX}e^{sY}=e^{tX+sY}
, finishing the proof.
∎

Lemma
3.20
fails if
t
and
s
are large. See the example below.

Example 3.21
.

Consider the vector fields
X(x,y)=(e^{-x}\cos y,-e^{-x}\sin y)
and
Y(x,y)=(e^{-x}\sin y,e^{-x}\cos y)
defined in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By direct computation,
[X,Y]=0
so
X
and
Y
commute.

One can see that we have the following ODE solutions, for
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle e^{tX}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(t^{2}+2t+2),\tfrac{3}{2}\pi+\arctan(t-1)),

\displaystyle\quad e^{2X}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{7}{4}\pi);

\displaystyle e^{sY}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(s^{2}+2s+2),\pi-\arctan(s-1)),

\displaystyle\quad e^{2Y}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{3}{4}\pi);

\displaystyle e^{sY}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{7}{4}\pi)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(s^{2}+2s+2),2\pi+\arctan(s-1)),

\displaystyle\quad e^{2Y}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{7}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{9}{4}\pi);

\displaystyle e^{tX}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{3}{4}\pi)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(t^{2}+2t+2),\tfrac{1}{2}\pi-\arctan(t-1)),

\displaystyle\quad e^{2X}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{3}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{1}{4}\pi).

Therefore
e^{2X}e^{2Y}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{9}{4}\pi)
and
e^{2Y}e^{2X}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{3}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{1}{4}\pi)
are not equal.

A related fact is that for the holomorphic vector field
Z(z)=e^{-z}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
defined on
\mathbb{C}^{1}
, we cannot talk about its holomoprhic ODE flow globally.

In the proof of Frobenius theorem we need Hölder-Zygmund regularity estimates for ODE flows as follows:

Lemma 3.22

(Flow regularity)
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,\infty]
and let
X
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector field on an open set
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then for any
U_{1}\Subset U_{0}
and
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})\cdot\|X\|_{C^{0}(U_{0})}^{-1}
, the map
\exp_{X}
is defined on
(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})\times U_{1}
and
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}((-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}),U_{1};U_{0})
.

See Lemma
A.2
for a paramter version. Here for
\alpha=\infty
we use the standard convention
\alpha+1=\infty
.

Proof.

We only need to prove the case
\alpha<\infty
.

Let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{0})
be such that
\chi\equiv 1
in a neighborhood of
\{x:\operatorname{dist}(x,U_{1})\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})\}
. We see that
e^{tX}|_{U_{1}}=e^{t(\chi X)}|_{U_{1}}
whenever
|t|<\varepsilon_{0}
. Therefore, in
U_{1}
we can replace
X
by
\chi X
in the discussion. In other words, we can assume
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Let
R>0
be such that
\operatorname{supp}X\subseteq B^{n}(0,R)
and let
\chi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B^{n}(0,2R))
be such that
\chi_{1}|_{B^{n}(0,R)}\equiv 1
. Clearly,
e^{tX}(x)=x
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
if
x\notin B^{n}(0,R)
. Thus
f(t,x):=\chi_{1}(x)\cdot e^{tX}(x)
solves the transport equation

\partial_{t}f(t,x)-(Xf)(t,x)=0,\qquad f(0,x)=\chi_{1}(x)\cdot x,\quad t\in\mathbb{R},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

By
[
BCD11
, Theorem 3.14]
we get
f\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}((-T,T),\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for every
T>0
. Clearly
f\in C^{1}([-T,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for every
T>0
and
f(t,x)\equiv e^{tX}(x)
whenever
|x|<R
. Thus
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,\alpha}((-T,T),B(0,R);\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The results
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}((-T,T),B(0,R);\mathbb{R}^{n})
follows from the following recursive argument: locally in
(-T,T)\times B(0,R)
,

\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{k,\alpha}\overunderset{\text{by Lemma \ref{Lem::Hold::QCompIFT} \ref{Item::Hold::QComp}}}{\text{or Lemma \ref{Lem::Hold::CompofMixHold} \ref{Item::Hold::CompofMixHold::Comp}}}{\Longrightarrow}\tfrac{\partial\exp_{X}}{\partial t}=X\circ\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,k),\alpha}\Rightarrow\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,k)+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,k)+1,\alpha}.

We start with
k=1
and ends at
k=\lceil\alpha\rceil
. Thus
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}((-T,T),B(0,R);\mathbb{R}^{n})
, finishing the proof.
∎

In applications, we consider the multiflow associated with commutative vector fields.

Corollary 3.23
.

Let
\alpha>1
and let
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r}]^{\top}
be a collection of commutative
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields on an open set
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
. Then for any
U_{1}\Subset U_{0}
there is a
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that we the following equality, both of whose sides are defined:

e^{t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{r}X_{r}}(x)=e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\circ\dots\circ e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(x),\quad\text{for }t=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r})\in(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})^{r},\quad x\in U_{1}.

Moreover by denoting
\exp_{X}(t,x):=e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(x)
, we have

\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}(B^{r}(0,\varepsilon_{0}),U_{1};U_{0})
and satisfies
\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}\exp_{X}(t,x)=X_{j}\circ\exp_{X}(t,x)
for
j=1,\dots,r
.

Proof.

We see that
\exp_{X}
is defined whenever
\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{r}\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})\cdot(\max_{1\leq j\leq r}\|X_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U_{0};\mathbb{R}^{n})})^{-1}
.

The property
e^{t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{r}X_{r}}(x)=e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}\exp_{X}=X_{j}\circ\exp_{X}
follows from Lemma
3.20
. The regularity statement
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}
follows from Lemma
3.22
.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

By Corollary
3.23
we can now use the multi-flow notation
e^{t\cdot X}=\exp_{X}(t,\cdot)
, where
t=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
is a small vector and
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r}]^{\top}
is a collection of (at least
C^{1}
) commutative vector fields.

All these properties of ODE flows also work for holomorphic vector fields on complex manifolds.

Lemma 3.25

(Holomorphic ODE flow with parameter)
.

Let
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
V_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be bounded open sets that are endowed with standard complex coordinate system
z=(z^{1},\dots,z^{m})
and real coordinate system
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
respectively.

Let
\beta>0
, and let
Z=(Z^{1},\dots,Z^{m}):U_{0}\times V_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-map that is holomorphic in
z
-variable. Then

(i)

For any
U_{1}\times V_{1}\Subset U_{0}\times V_{0}
there are an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a unique continuous map
\Phi^{Z}:\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{\tau}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}\to U_{0}
such that
\Phi^{Z}
is
C^{1}
in
(\tau,z)
and satisfies

\textstyle\frac{\partial\Phi^{Z}}{\partial\tau}(\tau,z,s)=Z(\Phi^{Z}(\tau,z,s)),\quad\frac{\partial\Phi^{Z}}{\partial\bar{\tau}}(\tau,z,s)=0,\quad\Phi^{Z}(0,z,s)=z,\quad\forall\tau\in\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2},\ z\in U_{1},\ s\in V_{1}.

(3.8)

In addition,
\Phi^{Z}
is holomorphic in
(\tau,z)
and
\Phi^{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

(ii)

Moreover, suppose
W:U_{0}\times V_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
is another such map as
Z
, such that two vector fields
\sum_{j=1}^{m}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}W^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}
are commutative. Let
\Phi^{Z}
and
\Phi^{W}
be as in (
3.8
). Then for any for any
U_{1}\times V_{1}\Subset U_{0}\times V_{0}
there are an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

\Phi^{Z}(\tau_{1},\Phi^{W}(\tau_{2},z,s),s)=\Phi^{W}(\tau_{2},\Phi^{Z}(\tau_{1},z,s),s),\quad\forall\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2},\quad z\in U_{1},\quad s\in V_{1}.

(3.9)

Remark 3.26
.

If we forget the
s
-variable, the result is indeed the well-definedness of the holomorphic ODE flow
e^{\tau Z}(z)
. In this way, we can write
\Phi^{Z}
as

\Phi^{Z}(\tau,z,s)=\exp_{Z(\cdot,s)}(\tau,z)=e^{\tau Z(\cdot,s)}(z).

(3.10)

And thus (
3.9
) is saying that
e^{\tau_{1}Z(\cdot,s)}e^{\tau_{2}Z(\cdot,s)}=e^{\tau_{2}Z(\cdot,s)}e^{\tau_{1}Z(\cdot,s)}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.25
.

Write
(x,y):=(\operatorname{Re}z,\operatorname{Im}z)
. We define real vector fields
X
and
Y
on
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2m}_{x,y}
with parameter on
V_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2m+q}_{s}
as

X(x,y;s)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\operatorname{Re}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\operatorname{Im}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}},\quad Y(x,y;s)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}-\operatorname{Im}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\operatorname{Re}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}.

(3.11)

By assumption,
Z
is holomorphic in
z
, so
[X,Y]=0
is commutative.

(i)
: By the well-definedness of
C^{1}
ODE flows and Corollary
3.23
, we can find a
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
\tilde{\Phi}(u,v,x,y,s):=e^{uX(\cdot,s)+vY(\cdot,s)}(x,y)
is the unique map defined in
(u,v)\in(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})^{2}
,
(x,y,s)\in U_{1}\times V_{1}
such that
\tilde{\Phi}
is
C^{1}
in
(u,v)
and satisfies
\partial_{u}\tilde{\Phi}=X\circ\tilde{\Phi}
,
\partial_{v}\tilde{\Phi}=Y\circ\tilde{\Phi}
and
\tilde{\Phi}(0,0,x,y,s)=(x,y,s)
.

By Lemma
A.2

(t,x,y,s)\mapsto\exp_{X(\cdot,s)}(t,(x,y))
and
(t,x,y,s)\mapsto\exp_{Y(\cdot,s)}(t,(x,y))
both belongs to
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{(t,x,y),s}
. Since
\tilde{\Phi}(u,v,x,y,s):=\exp_{X(\cdot,s)}(u,\exp_{Y(\cdot,s)}(v,(x,y)))
, by Lemma
2.32

(i)
, we get
\tilde{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{(u,v,x,y),s}
as well.

We can write
\tilde{\Phi}=(\tilde{\Phi}^{R},\tilde{\Phi}^{I})
where
\tilde{\Phi}^{R},\tilde{\Phi}^{I}:(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
are the components of
x
and
y
-coordinates respectively. Thus, by direct computation we see that
\Phi^{Z}:=\tilde{\Phi}^{R}+i\tilde{\Phi}^{I}
solves (
3.8
).

The uniqueness of
\Phi^{Z}
follows from the uniqueness of
\tilde{\Phi}
.
On the other hand, by the uniqueness of the holomorphic ODE flow (see
[
IY08
, Theorem 1.1]
for example), we know
\Phi^{Z}(\cdot,s)
is the holomoprhic ODE flow for the holomorphic vector field
Z(\cdot,s)
, for each
s
. Thus
\Phi^{Z}
is holomorphic in
z
as well.

Now
\tilde{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{u,v,x,y,s}
, so
\Phi^{Z}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-map. By Lemma
2.19
we get
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\tau,z}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
.

(ii)
: We can fix a
s\in V_{1}
in the discussion below, thus
Z
and
W
are holomorphic vector fields in
U_{0}
.

Let
W=(W^{1},\dots,W^{m})
be from the assumption, we define vector fields
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
to be the “real and imaginary part of
W
” by replacing
Z^{j}
with
W^{j}
in (
3.11
). We have
[X,Y]=0
and
[X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}]=0
since
Z
and
W
are holormorphic in
z
. The commutativity of
\sum_{j=1}^{m}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}W^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}
implies that
X,X^{\prime},Y,Y^{\prime}
are all pairwise commutative.

By Corollary
3.23
we have
e^{u_{1}X}e^{v_{1}Y}e^{u_{2}X^{\prime}}e^{v_{2}Y^{\prime}}=e^{u_{2}X^{\prime}}e^{v_{2}Y^{\prime}}e^{u_{1}X}e^{v_{1}Y}
in
U_{1}\times V_{1}
for
u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
small. Repeating the argument for the construction of
\Phi^{Z}
above we get (
3.9
), finishing the proof.
∎

3.3
The bi-layer Frobenius theorem and a holomorphic analogy

The classical Frobenius theorem states that an involutive real tangent subbundle is locally spanned by coordinate vector fields in a suitable real coordinate system. In this subsection, we will prove some generalizations along with the Hölder-Zygmund regularity estimates.

In the proof of Theorem
1.2
(or Theorem
6.2
), we need to apply the real Frobenius theorem on
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and on
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
simultaneously. In order to achieve the best possible regularity, we need a slightly different proof.

In the following, we endow
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard coordinate system
t=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r})
,
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{m})
and
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
respectively. For the case
\kappa=\infty
, we use the convention
\kappa-1=\infty
.

Theorem 3.27

(Bi-layer Frobenius theorem)
.

Let
\kappa\in(2,\infty]
and
\beta,\gamma\in(1,\kappa-1]
. Let
r,m,q
be non-negative integers. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
(r+m+q)
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold. Let
\mathcal{V}\leq\mathcal{E}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
be two involutive real tangent subbundles such that
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
has rank
r
, and
\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
has rank
r+m
.

Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, there is a neighborhood
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
of
(0,0,0)
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma,\beta)}
-regular parameterization
\Phi(t,x,s):\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
, such that:

(1)

\Phi(0)=p
.

(2)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{t,x,s}^{\gamma+1,\min(\gamma,\beta+1),\min(\gamma,\beta)}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma,\beta+1),\min(\gamma,\beta)}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma-1,\beta)}(\Omega;T\mathfrak{M})
.

(3)

For every
(t,x,s)\in\Omega
,
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(t,x,s)}
is spanned by
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,x,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,x,s)\in T_{\Phi(t,x,s)}\mathfrak{M}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{\Phi(t,x,s)}
is spanned by
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,x,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,x,s),\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{1}}(t,x,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{m}}(t,x,s)\in T_{\Phi(t,x,s)}\mathfrak{M}
.

On the coordinates side, set
U:=\Phi(\Omega)\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
and let
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be the inverse map of
\Phi
. Then we have

(4)

F(p)=0
.

(5)

F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U;\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(6)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U;T\mathfrak{M})
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma-1,\beta)}(U;T\mathfrak{M})
.

(7)

\mathcal{V}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
, and
\mathcal{E}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}
.

For definition of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
-type maps, see Definitions
2.15

(iv)
and
3.2
.

Remark 3.28
.

Here the regularity results for
F
and those for
\Phi
do not directly imply each other.

If we take
m=0
in Theorem
3.27
, i.e.
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{E}
, we get the Hölder estimate immediately for the classical real Frobenius theorem.

Corollary 3.29

(Real Frobenius theorem)
.

Let
\kappa\in(2,\infty]
,
\alpha\in(1,\kappa-1]
, let
r,q\geq 0
, and let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
(r+q)
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold. Assume
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-involutive real tangent subbundle of rank
r
.

Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
0
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-regular parameterization
\Phi(t,s):\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
, such that:

(1)

\Phi(0)=p
.

(2)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{t,s}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}(\Omega;\mathfrak{M})
, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{(t,s)}(\Omega;T\mathfrak{M})
.

(3)

For any
(t,s)\in\Omega
,
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(t,s)}\leq T_{\Phi(t,s)}\mathfrak{M}
is spanned by
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,s)
.

On the coordinate side, set
U:=\Phi(\Omega)\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
and let
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}):=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
, we have:

(4)

F(p)=0
.

(5)

F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
and
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(6)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;T\mathfrak{M})
.

(7)

\mathcal{V}|_{U}
has a local basis
(F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}})
.

Corollary
3.29
is sharp, see Section
7
and Proposition
7.1
.

Proof of the Bi-layer Frobenius, Theorem
3.27
.

We can pick an initial
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-coordinate system near
p

(u,v,w)=(u^{1},\dots,u^{r},v^{1},\dots,v^{m},w^{1},\dots,w^{q}):U_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q},

(3.12)

such that

•

u(p)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
v(p)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
w(p)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
;

•

\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{r}}|_{p}
form a real basis for
\mathcal{V}_{p}\leq T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
;

•

\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{r}}|_{p},\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{m}}|_{p}
form a real basis for
\mathcal{E}_{p}\leq T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
.

Thus, we can identify
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}
with their pushforwards on the open neighborhood of
(0,0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
. In other words, we can assume
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{u}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{w}
and
p=(0,0,0)
.

Applying Lemma
3.9
, on
\mathcal{V}
and on
\mathcal{E}
respectively, we can find a smaller neighborhood
U_{1}\subseteq U_{0}
of
p=0
, a
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-local basis
T=[T_{1},\dots,T_{r}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{V}
on
U_{1}
, and a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-local basis
\tilde{X}=[X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}]^{\top}=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{E}
on
U_{1}
, that have the following form:

T=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+A^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v}+A^{\prime\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial w},\quad\tilde{X}=\begin{pmatrix}X^{\prime}\\
X^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}&&B^{\prime}\\
&I_{m}&B^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial w}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{on }U_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{u}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{w}.

(3.13)

Here
A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r\times m})
,
A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r\times n})
,
B^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r\times n})
and
B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{m\times n})
.

By (
3.13
) we see that
T_{1},\dots,T_{r},X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are linearly independent, so they form a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma,\beta)}
-local basis for
\mathcal{E}
on
U_{1}
.

We define a natural map
\Gamma
as:

\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{u}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{w},\qquad\Gamma(s):=(0,0,s).

(3.14)

We have
\Gamma(0)=(0,0,0)=p
, and
\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial s^{j}}(s)=\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{j}}\big|_{\Gamma(s)}
