\alpha
or
\beta=\infty
, without any change of the proof.

For the application to Theorem
1.3
, we only need the contraposition of
(A)

\Rightarrow

(B)
.

Proof.

(A)

\Rightarrow

(B)
: Write
F=:(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})
and
\Phi=(\Phi^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime}):=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}:F(U_{1}\times V_{1})\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
. By assumption
\nabla_{z}\Phi=(\nabla_{z}\Phi^{\prime},\nabla_{z}\Phi^{\prime\prime})\in C^{0}(F(U_{1}\times V_{1});\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
, so
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\frac{\partial(\operatorname{Re}\Phi^{\prime},\operatorname{Im}\Phi^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime})}{\partial z}\circ F
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}=\frac{\partial(\operatorname{Re}\Phi^{\prime},\operatorname{Im}\Phi^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime})}{\partial\bar{z}}\circ F
are well-defined continuous vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{(\operatorname{Re}w,\operatorname{Im}w,\theta)}
.

By assumption
(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{U_{1}\times V_{1}}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w},\partial_{\bar{w}})
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
, so
\partial_{w}F^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{\bar{w}}F^{\prime\prime}=0
in the sense of distributions, which means
F^{\prime\prime}(w,\theta)=F^{\prime\prime}(\theta)
.

By assumption
d\bar{F}^{\prime}=F^{*}d\bar{z}
is continuous in
w
and is annihilated by
\operatorname{Span}F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})
. Therefore

(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})\bar{F}^{\prime}=\bar{F}^{\prime}_{w}+a\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}=0,\quad\text{i.e.}\quad F^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}=-\bar{a}F^{\prime}_{w}.

(7.6)

So for the transition matrix (cf. (
7.3
)) we have

\begin{pmatrix}F^{*}dz\\
F^{*}d\bar{z}\\
F^{*}ds\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime}_{w}&-\bar{a}F^{\prime}_{w}&F^{\prime}_{\theta}\\
-a\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\theta}\\
&&F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}dw\\
d\bar{w}\\
d\theta\end{pmatrix}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime}_{w}&-a\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
-\bar{a}F^{\prime}_{w}&\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F^{*}\partial_{z}\\
F^{*}\partial_{\bar{z}}\end{pmatrix}.

(7.7)

Applying the Cramer’s rule of inverse matrices to the right hand matrix in (
7.7
), we have

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}}{(1-|a|^{2})|F^{\prime}_{w}|^{2}}(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})=\frac{\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}}}{(1-|a|^{2})F^{\prime}_{w}}\quad\text{in}\quad U_{1}\times V_{1}.

(7.8)

Since
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{3})
and
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C})
, we see that
F^{\prime}_{w}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C})
and
F^{\prime}_{w}\not\equiv 0
in
U_{1}
.

Take
U_{2}\times V_{2}\subseteq U_{1}\times V_{1}
as a neighborhood of
(0,0)
such that
\sup_{(w,\theta)\in U_{2}\times V_{2}}|\frac{F^{\prime}_{w}(w,\theta)}{F^{\prime}_{w}(0,\theta)}-1|<1
.
We define

f(w,\theta):=\log\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}(w,\theta)}{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}(0,\theta)}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\Big(\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}(w,\theta)}{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}(0,\theta)}-1\Big)^{n}.

Since
\big[(w,\theta)\mapsto\frac{F^{\prime}_{w}(w,\theta)}{F^{\prime}_{w}(0,\theta)}\big]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
, we see that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
as well. So (
7.5
) follows:

f_{w}+af_{\bar{w}}=\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{w\bar{w}}}{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}}+\frac{a\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}\bar{w}}}{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}}=\frac{(\bar{F}^{\prime}_{w}+a\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}})_{\bar{w}}}{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}}-\frac{a_{\bar{w}}\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}}{\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}}=-a_{\bar{w}}.

(7.9)

(B)

\Rightarrow

(A)
: This is the reverse of the above argument. By shrinking
U_{2}\times V_{2}
we assume
U_{2}
and
V_{2}
are both convex neighborhoods.

Suppose
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
is a solution to (
7.5
). Take
\mu:=e^{f}
, we know
\mu\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
satisfies

(a\mu)_{\bar{w}}=a_{\bar{w}}e^{f}+af_{\bar{w}}e^{f}=-f_{w}e^{f}=-\mu_{w},\quad\text{in}\quad U_{2}\times V_{2}.

(7.10)

Therefore
\mu(\cdot,\theta)(d\bar{w}-a(\cdot,\theta)dw)
is a closed 1-form in
U_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}
, for each
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. We define
F^{\prime}:U_{2}\times V_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
as

F^{\prime}(w,\theta):=\int_{0}^{1}(w\cdot\bar{\mu}(tw,\theta)+\bar{w}\cdot\bar{a}(tw,\theta)\bar{\mu}(tw,\theta))dt.

(7.11)

We see that
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C})
and satisfies
d\bar{F}^{\prime}=\mu d\bar{w}-a\mu dw+\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\theta}d\theta
. In other words

\nabla_{w}(F^{\prime},\bar{F}^{\prime})=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime}_{w}&\bar{F}^{\prime}_{w}\\
F^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\mu}&-a\mu\\
-\bar{a}\bar{\mu}&\mu\end{pmatrix}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\det\big(\nabla_{w}(F^{\prime},\bar{F}^{\prime})\big)=|\mu|^{2}(1-|a|^{2})>0.

Since
a,\mu\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
, we see that
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}
and the matrix function
\nabla_{w}(F^{\prime},\bar{F}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
is invertible at
(0,0)
. Applying Lemma
2.32

(iii)
to
(\operatorname{Re}F^{\prime},\operatorname{Im}F^{\prime})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we can find a neighborhood
U_{1}\times V_{1}\subseteq U_{2}\times V_{2}
such that
F^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta):U_{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}
is injective for each
\theta\in V_{1}
and its inverse map
\Phi^{\prime}(z,s)=F^{\prime}(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(z)
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{z,s}
near
(F^{\prime}(0,0),0)=(0,0)
.

Take
F(w,\theta):=(F^{\prime}(w,\theta),\theta)
and
\Phi:=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}
, we see that
\Phi(z,s)=(\Phi^{\prime}(z,s),s)
. Therefore
F:U_{1}\times V_{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
is homeomorphic to its image (since
F,\Phi
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}\subset C^{0}
), and
\nabla_{z}(F^{\mathrm{Inv}})=(\nabla_{z}\Phi^{\prime},0)
is a continuous map on
F(U_{1}\times V_{1})
. This completes the proof of
(A)(A.1)
.

Now for each
\theta\in V_{1}
,
F^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta):U_{1}\to\mathbb{C}_{z}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate chart satisfying
d(\bar{F}^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta))=\mu(\cdot,\theta)d\bar{w}-a\mu(\cdot,\theta)dw
in
U_{1}
, so
(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})F^{\prime}=0
, which means
F^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta)^{*}d\bar{z}=d\bar{F}^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta)
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U_{1}\times\{\theta\}}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}+a(\cdot,\theta)\partial_{\bar{w}})
. Taking a dual we get
\mathcal{S}|_{U_{1}\times\{\theta\}}=\operatorname{Span}F^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta)^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
for each
\theta\in V_{1}
.

Now
F(w,\theta)=(F^{\prime}(w,\theta),\theta)
and
\Phi(z,s)=(\Phi^{\prime}(z,s),s)
, so
((\nabla_{z}\Phi)\circ F)(w,\theta)=((\nabla_{z}\Phi^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta))\circ F^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta))(w)
hence
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}|_{(w,\theta)}=F^{\prime}(\cdot,\theta)^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}|_{w}
spans
\mathcal{S}_{(w,\theta)}
for each
w\in U_{1}
and
\theta\in V_{2}
, finishing the proof of
(A)(A.2)
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

We do not know how to relax the assumption
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
to
\alpha>0
. The difficulty comes from (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) in the proof, where the intermediate calculations need a product of a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
-function and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-function. On the other hand, the PDE (
7.5
) can still be defined when
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2}
, by requiring
a
to stay in the space, for example,
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w},\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta};\mathbb{C})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta};W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w};\mathbb{C}))
(see Definition
7.5
and Proposition
7.9

(i)
).

For Step 2, the construction of
a(w,\theta)
requires the following.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
k\geq 0
be an integer,
\beta>0
,
1\leq p<\infty
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
be an open set and let
(\psi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution of
\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
. We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{q};W^{k,p}_{x}(U))
the space of all distributions
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}\times U_{x})
such that
\psi_{j}\ast_{s}f(\cdot,s)\in W^{k,p}(U)
for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
(recall Notation
2.5
for
\psi_{j}\ast_{s}f
), with

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q};W^{k,p}(U))}=\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(W^{k,p})}:=\sup_{j\geq 0,s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}2^{j\beta}\|(\psi_{j}\ast_{s}f)(\cdot,s)\|_{W^{k,p}(U)}<\infty.

We denote by
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{q};W^{k,p}(U))
the space of all
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-function
f:\mathbb{R}^{q}\times U\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(\cdot,s)\in W^{k,p}(U)
for almost every
s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
with
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(W^{k,p})}:=\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{W^{k,p}(U)}<\infty
.

One can check that
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q};W^{k,p}(U))
is the same as putting
X=W^{k,p}(U)
in Definition
1.8
(see also Remark
2.7
). In particular the space does not depend on the choice of
(\psi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
. We leave their proof to readers since we are allowed to fix a choice of
(\psi_{j})_{j}
in the later discussion.

When
U
is a smooth domain, the space
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{q};W^{k,p}(U))
coincides with the classical vector-valued
L^{\infty}
-space (cf. Remark
2.7

(iv)
) since
W^{k,p}(U)
is a separable space.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\beta>0
and
1<p<\infty
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded smooth domain.

(i)

The product map
[(f,g)\mapsto fg]:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q};L^{p}(U))\times L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q};L^{p}(U))
is bounded bilinear.

(ii)

When
\beta<1
, the product map
[(f,g)\mapsto fg]:(L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2})(U)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U)
is bounded bilinear.

Proof.

(i)
can follow from the boundedness of the product map
L^{p}(U)\times L^{\infty}(U)\to L^{p}(U)
and that
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
is closed under multiplications. More precisely, by Lemma
2.24
we have the following uniformly in
l\geq 0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
:

\displaystyle\|(\psi_{l}\ast_{s}(fg))(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{p}(U)}\leq\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\big(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\big)\|(\psi_{j}\ast_{s}f)(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{p}(U)}\|(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{s}g)(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{\psi}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(L^{p}_{x})}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}}\big(\sum_{|j-l|\geq 2}\sum_{j^{\prime}\geq j-3}+\sum_{|j^{\prime}-l|\geq 2}\sum_{j\geq j^{\prime}-3}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\big)2^{-j\beta}2^{-j^{\prime}\beta}\lesssim_{\beta}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(L^{p}_{x})}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}}2^{-l\beta}.

Proof of
(ii)
follows from
[
BCD11
, Chapter 2.8.1]
, where in the reference we use Bony’s decomposition
uv=T(u,v)+T(v,u)+R(u,v)
for distributions
u,v
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(see
[
BCD11
, (2.41)]
and Remark
2.25
). By
[
BCD11
, Theorem 2.82]
we have
T:L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
T:\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. By
[
BCD11
, Theorem 2.85]
we have
R:W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{B}_{22}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Here
\mathscr{B}_{22}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=H^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the fractional
L^{2}
-Sobolev space (see for example
[
BCD11
, Definition 2.68]
or
[
Tri10
, Definition 2.3.1/2(i)]
).
We have Sobolev embedding
\mathscr{B}_{22}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, whose proof can be found in for example
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.7.1]
6
6

6

We recall the correspondence of Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q})=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
and Triebel-Lizorkin space
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathscr{F}_{p2}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
from
[
Tri10
, Theorems 2.5.7 and 2.5.12]
.
. Thus
R:W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is bounded.

Therefore
[(u,v)\mapsto uv]:(L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is bilinearly bounded. Taking restrictions to
U
we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}
be the zero Dirichlet boundary solution operators in Lemma
2.31
with
U=\mathbb{B}^{2}_{w}\subset\mathbb{C}^{1}
and
V=\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
in the lemma. Define
\mathcal{T}_{0}:=4\partial_{\bar{w}}\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{T}:=4\partial_{\bar{w}}\mathcal{P}
. Then

(i)

\partial_{w}\mathcal{T}f=f
for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{T}
has the following boundedness: for
\alpha>0
,
1<p<\infty
and
\mathscr{X}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}:\gamma>0\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2}))\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2})).

(7.12)

(ii)

Moreover, if
f\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
satisfies
f|_{\{|w|>\frac{1}{2}\}}\equiv 0
, then

f-\mathcal{T}\partial_{w}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C}).

(7.13)

Proof.

That
\partial_{w}\mathcal{T}=\operatorname{id}
and
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{w}\mathscr{X}_{\theta}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{w}\mathscr{X}_{\theta}
are the immediate consequences to Lemma
2.31
since
\Delta_{\operatorname{Re}w,\operatorname{Im}w}=4\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial w\partial\bar{w}}
.

We have boundedness
\mathcal{P}_{0}:L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2})\to W^{2,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2})
by
[
GT01
, Theorem 9.13]
, thus
\mathcal{T}_{0}:L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
. We get
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(L^{p})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(W^{1,p})
by the same reason to Lemma
2.9
. Thus we prove
(i)
.

To prove
(ii)
, we need to show that
\operatorname{id}-\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}:L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{M}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
is bounded for all
M>0
.

Let
g\in L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
with zero extension outside
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}
. By formula of Green functions (see for example
[
Eva10
, Theorem 2.12 and Section 2.2.c]
) we have for
w\in\mathbb{B}^{2}
,

g(w)-\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}g(w)=g(w)-\int_{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}}4g(z)\partial^{2}_{z\bar{w}}\big(\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-w)-\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(|z|w-\tfrac{z}{|z|}\big)\big)dxdy=\int_{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}}4g(z)\partial^{2}_{z\bar{w}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(|z|w-\tfrac{z}{|z|}\big)dxdy.

Here
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{B}^{2}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|z|
(which is the
n=2
case in (
B.1
)). The first equality follows from integration by part, the second equality follows from the fact that
\partial_{z\bar{w}}\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-w)=\partial_{z\bar{z}}\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-w)=\frac{1}{4}\Delta_{x,y}\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-w)=\frac{\delta_{w}(z)}{4}
.

Clearly
(z,w)\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}(|z|w-z/|z|)
is a smooth function on
(z,w)\in r\mathbb{B}^{2}\times\mathbb{B}^{2}
for any
r<1
. Thus for any integer
M\geq 0

\|\nabla^{M}(g-\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}g)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2^{k}})}\leq\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})}\sup_{|w|<\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{2}}\big|\nabla^{M+2}_{z,w}\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(|z|w-\tfrac{z}{|z|}\big)\big|dxdy\lesssim_{M}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})}.

We conclude that
\operatorname{id}-\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}:L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{M}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
is bounded for all
M>0
. By Lemma
2.9
, we get the boundedness
\operatorname{id}-\mathcal{T}\partial_{w}:L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{M}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. The proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
\beta>0
and let
(c_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{C}
be a sequence.
Define
g(\theta):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}e^{2\pi i\cdot 2^{k}\theta}2^{-\beta\theta}
, then

(i)

\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})}\approx\sup_{k\geq 0}|c_{k}|
with the implied constant depending only on
\beta
.

(ii)

If
\varlimsup_{k\to\infty}|c_{k}|=\infty
, then
g\notin\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(I;\mathbb{C})
for every interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
\{\psi_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution for
\mathbb{R}
. Recall from (
2.1
)
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{k}\subset(-2^{k-1},-2^{k+1})\cup(2^{k-1},2^{k+1})
holds for
k\geq 1
. Since the Fourier support of
e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}
is
\{2^{k}\}
, we have
\psi_{k}\ast g(\theta)=c_{k}2^{-\beta\theta}e^{2\pi i\cdot 2^{k}\theta}
for all
k\geq 1
. Therefore by Lemma
2.2

(i)
, we get the result
(i)
:

\textstyle\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}\approx_{\psi,\beta}\sup_{k\geq 0}2^{k\beta}\|\psi_{k}\ast g\|_{L^{\infty}}=\sup_{k\geq 1}|c_{k}|.

Now suppose
\varlimsup_{k\to\infty}|c_{k}|=+\infty
, so
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}\approx_{\psi,\beta}=\sup|c_{k}|=\infty
.

For a given interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
,
I
contains a dyadic subinterval, say
[n2^{-k_{0}},(n+1)2^{-k_{0}}]
where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Take
g^{k_{0}}(\theta):=\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{\infty}c_{k}2^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}2^{-k\beta}
, so

\textstyle g(\theta)-g^{k_{0}}(\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}c_{k}e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}2^{-\beta\theta},

must have finite
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})
-norm (though whose bound depends on
k_{0}
).

Since
g^{k_{0}}
is
2^{-k_{0}}
-periodic,
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}=\infty
and
\|g-g^{k_{0}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
, we know
\|g^{k_{0}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(n2^{-k_{0}},(n+1)2^{-k_{0}})}=\infty
. Therefore, we complete the proof of
(ii)
by:

\textstyle\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(I)}\geq\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}([n2^{-k_{0}},(n+1)2^{-k_{0}}])}\geq\|g^{k_{0}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}([n2^{-k_{0}},(n+1)2^{-k_{0}}])}-\|g-g^{k_{0}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}=\infty.\qed

We now construct our
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-function
a(w,\theta)
and finish the Step 2:

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
. There is a function
A(w,\theta)
defined in
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
, that satisfies the following properties:

(i)

A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
and
[(w,\theta)\mapsto w\partial_{\bar{w}}A(w,\theta)]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
.

(ii)

[(w,\theta)\mapsto w^{-1}A(w,\theta)]\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\beta}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
for all
1<p<\frac{4}{2-\min(\alpha,2)}
. In particular
w^{-1}A\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\beta}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{\frac{\alpha+4}{2}})
.

(iii)

\mathcal{T}[\partial_{\bar{w}}A]\notin L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
near
(w,\theta)=(0,0)
, where
\mathcal{T}
be the inverse
\partial_{w}
-operator in Lemma
7.7
.

The construction is essentially the parameter version of
[
Yao21
]
.

Remark 7.10
.

By taking some simple modifications from the construction below (see (
7.15
)), one can also prove Proposition
7.9
for the case
\alpha=\infty
,
\beta<\infty
. We leave the details to readers.

Proof of Proposition
7.9
.

First we find a function
b\in C_{c}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
such that,

(b.1)

b\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
,
b\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
and
wb\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
.

(b.2)

\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}b\notin L^{\infty}
near
w=0
. Here
\mathcal{T}_{0}
be the inverse
\partial_{w}
-operator in Lemma
7.7
.

Indeed, we take a function
b
such that

b(w):=\partial_{w}\big(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{3}}\big)=-\tfrac{1}{6}(-\log|w|)^{-\frac{2}{3}}\tfrac{|w|^{2}}{w^{2}},\quad\text{when }|w|<\tfrac{1}{2}.

(7.14)

And
b
is smoothly cutoffed outside
|w|<\frac{1}{2}
.

Immediately we get
b\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
and
wb(w)\in C^{1}_{c}
. And clearly we have
|\nabla b(w)|\lesssim|w|^{-1}(-\log|w|)^{-\frac{2}{3}}
, so
b\in W^{1,2}
, which gives
(b.1)
.

To prove
(b.2)
, since
\partial_{w}\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}b=\partial_{\bar{w}}b
it suffices to show that if
U
is a neighborhood of
0
and
f:U\subseteq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
is a function satisfies
\partial_{w}f=\partial_{\bar{w}}b
, then
f\notin L^{\infty}
near
0
.

Indeed by (
7.14
) in a neighborhood of
0
we have
f_{w}=(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{2}})_{w\bar{w}}
, so
f-(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{2}})_{\bar{w}}
is annihilated by
\partial_{w}
which is an anti-holmorphic function. Therefore
f-(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{2}})_{\bar{w}}
is a bounded function near
w=0
. On the other hand

(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{3}})_{\bar{w}}=(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{3}}+O(1)\quad\text{as }w\to 0,

is not bounded near
0
. We conclude that
f\notin L^{\infty}
near
0
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w})
be a Schwartz function whose Fourier transform satisfies
\hat{\rho}_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2})
and
\hat{\rho}_{0}|_{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}}\equiv 1
. Define functions
(\rho_{k},c_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
and
A(w,\theta)
as

\rho_{k}(w):=2^{n\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}\rho_{0}(2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}w),\quad c_{k}(w):=\rho_{k}\ast b(w)-\rho_{k}\ast b(0),\quad k\geq 0;\quad A(w,\theta):=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}(w)e^{2\pi i2^{j}\theta}2^{-j\beta}.

(7.15)

Thus, we have
wc_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{C}}(w-z)\rho_{k}(w-z)b(z)d\operatorname{vol}(z)=(w\rho_{k})\ast b
for
k\geq 0
.
By scaling

\|\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}},\quad\|\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}},\quad\|w\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=\|w\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\quad\text{for each }k\geq 1.

(7.16)

Therefore,

\begin{gathered}\|c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\leq 2\|\rho_{k}\ast b\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\lesssim\|\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\|b\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\lesssim 1;\\
\|w\partial_{\bar{w}}c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|\partial_{\bar{w}}(w\rho_{k})\ast b\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|w\partial_{\bar{w}}\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\|b\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1.\end{gathered}

(7.17)

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
and
(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
be two dyadic resolutions for
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}
and
\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
respectively (recall Definition
2.1
). Recall from (
2.1
)
\hat{\phi}_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(2\mathbb{B}^{2})
,
\hat{\psi}_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(2\mathbb{B}^{1})
,
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j}\subset 2^{j+1}\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash 2^{j-1}\mathbb{B}^{2}
and
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{j}\subset 2^{j+1}\mathbb{B}^{1}\backslash 2^{j-1}\mathbb{B}^{1}
for
j\geq 1
. Since
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\rho}_{0}\subset\mathbb{B}^{2}
, by scaling we have
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\rho}_{k}\subset 2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}\mathbb{B}^{2}
. Thus,

\phi_{j}\ast_{w}A(w,\theta)=\sum_{k\geq\max(0,\frac{\alpha}{\beta}(j-1))}(\phi_{j}\ast c_{k})(w)e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}2^{-k\beta},\quad\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}A(w,\theta)=c_{j}(w)e^{2\pi i2^{j}\theta}2^{-j\beta},\quad j\geq 0.

(7.18)

By (
7.17
) and (
7.18
) we get
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
, since for
j\geq 0
,

\begin{gathered}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{w}A\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}\leq\sum_{k\geq\max(0,\frac{\alpha}{\beta}(j-1))}\|\phi_{j}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{1})}\|c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}2^{-k\beta}\lesssim_{\phi,c,\alpha,\beta}2^{-j\alpha};\\
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}A(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}\leq\|\psi_{j}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\|c_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}2^{-j\beta}\lesssim_{\psi,c,\beta}2^{-j\beta}.\end{gathered}

Taking supremum over
j\geq 0
we get
(i)
.

Using (
7.16
) and (
7.17
) we also have
\|c_{k}\|_{C^{1}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}
and
\|w\nabla c_{k}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim 1
. Thus

|\nabla c_{k}(w)|\lesssim\min(|w|^{-1},2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k})\leq|w|^{\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},1)-1}2^{k\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},1)}\leq|w|^{\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},1)-1}2^{k\frac{\beta}{2}},\quad\text{for all}\quad k\geq 0,\quad w\in\mathbb{C}^{1}.

By (
7.15
) we have
c_{k}(0,\theta)\equiv 0
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus by (
7.18
),

|\psi_{k}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}A)(w,\theta)|=|w^{-1}c_{k}(w)|2^{-k\beta}\leq|\nabla c_{k}(w)|2^{-k\beta}\leq|w|^{\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},1)-1}2^{-k\frac{\beta}{2}},\quad k\geq 0,\quad w\in\mathbb{C}^{2},\quad\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}.

On the other hand
|w|^{-\gamma}\in L^{\frac{2}{\gamma}-}(\mathbb{B}^{2})
holds for all
0<\gamma<2
. Thus for
1<p<\frac{2}{1-\min(\alpha/2,1)}=\frac{4}{2-\min(\alpha,2)}
we have
\sup_{k\geq 0;\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}2^{k\frac{\beta}{2}}\|\psi_{k}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}A)(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}<\infty
.

Clearly
2<\frac{\alpha+4}{2}<\frac{4}{2-\min(\alpha,2)}
, we finish the proof of
(ii)
.

To prove
(iii)
, since
\mathcal{T}
only acts on
w
-variable, by (
7.15
) we have

\mathcal{T}A_{\bar{w}}(w,\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k\alpha}e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}c_{k}(w)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k\alpha}e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}\mathcal{T}_{0}(\rho_{k}\ast\partial_{\bar{w}}b)(w),\quad w\in\mathbb{C}^{1},\ \theta\in\mathbb{R}.

Now
\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\rho_{k}\ast(\partial_{\bar{w}}b)=\partial_{\bar{w}}b
holds (as
L^{2}
-functions), and by
(b.2)

\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}b\notin L^{\infty}
near
w=0
. So for any neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}
of
w=0
, we have
\varlimsup_{k\to\infty}\|\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{C})}=+\infty
. Therefore by Lemma
7.8

(ii)
, for any interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
containing
\theta=0
, we have
\|\mathcal{T}\partial_{\bar{w}}A\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U,I;\mathbb{C})}\gtrsim\varlimsup_{k\to\infty}\|\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}b\|_{L^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{C})}=+\infty
.

So
\mathcal{T}\partial_{\bar{w}}A\notin L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
near
(0,0)
, finishing the proof of
(iii)
.
∎

Now we prove Step 3, which is the non-existence of
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
to (
7.5
). Cf.
[
Yao21
, Proposition 7]
. Here we consider all
\alpha,\beta>0
.

Theorem 7.11

(
\bar{\partial}
-equation for sharpness of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
, let
A
be as in Proposition
7.9
. Then there is a constant
\delta=\delta(\alpha,\beta,A)>0
, such that for any neighborhood
U_{2}\times V_{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
of
(0,0)
there is no
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
solving (
7.5
) with
a(w,\theta):=\delta\cdot A(w,\theta)
.

This result contains the largest possible range for
\alpha
and
\beta
. The proof can be simplified if
\alpha>1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
be the inverse
\partial_{w}
-operator as in Lemma
7.7
. Recall that
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
is also bounded for
1<p<\infty
.

We define a linear operator
\mathfrak{T}=\mathfrak{T}_{A}
on functions in
\mathbb{B}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{1}
as

\mathfrak{T}[\varphi]:=\mathcal{T}(A\cdot\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi),\quad\varphi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C}).

(7.19)

By Lemma
2.26

(i)
, Lemma
7.6

(i)
and
(ii)
,

\displaystyle\|A\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}\|\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}\|\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})},

\displaystyle\text{provided }\alpha>\tfrac{1}{2};

\displaystyle\|A\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}\|A\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2})}\|\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}\|\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})},

\displaystyle\text{provided }0<\alpha<1;

\displaystyle\|A\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\beta}\|A\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))}\lesssim_{A,p}\|\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))},

\displaystyle\text{for all }1<p<\infty.

Taking
p=p_{\alpha}:=4+16/\alpha
, we conclude that
\mathfrak{T}
is a bounded endomorphism on both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
.

We now take
\delta=\delta(\alpha,\beta,A)>0
small such that

\delta\big(\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}+\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+16/\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))}\big)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

(7.20)

Note that
\mathfrak{T}
is now contraction map in these two spaces.

We claim that
a(w,\theta)=\delta A(w,\theta)
is as desired.

Suppose by contradiction there is a
U_{2}\times V_{2}\ni(0,0)
and a
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
solving (
7.5
). Take
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
such that
\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\subseteq U_{2}
and
(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})\subseteq V_{2}
. We define
\tilde{\chi}
and
\chi
such that

\tilde{\chi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}),\quad\tilde{\chi}|_{(-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{0},\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{0})}\equiv 1,\quad\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})),\quad\chi(w,\theta):=\tilde{\chi}(|w|)\tilde{\chi}(\theta).

(7.21)

Therefore
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{2})
equals to 1 in the neighborhood
\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times(-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2})
of
(0,0)
, and
\nabla_{w}\chi
vanishes in the neighborhood
\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
of
\{w=0\}=\{0\}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
.

Define
g,h\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{c}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
as
g:=\chi f
and
h(w,\theta):=wg(w,\theta)
, so

g_{w}+a\cdot g_{\bar{w}}=(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi a_{\bar{w}},\quad h_{w}+a\cdot h_{\bar{w}}=g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}},\quad\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{2}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}.

(7.22)

Claim 1:

h\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
and therefore
h_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
.

Rewriting the second equation of (
7.22
) we have

h=-\mathcal{T}(ah_{\bar{w}})+(h-\mathcal{T}h_{w})+\mathcal{T}(g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}}).

In other words
h=\varphi
is a solution to the following affine linear equation:

\varphi=-\delta\mathfrak{T}[\varphi]+(h-\mathcal{T}h_{w})+\mathcal{T}(g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}}).

(7.23)

By assumption
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
,
f\nabla_{w}\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C}^{2})
. By Proposition
7.9

(i)

wa_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. Therefore by (
7.12
) (using
L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}(L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}_{w})
) we get

\mathcal{T}(g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}})\in\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C}))\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})).

(7.24)

By assumption
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\subset L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
is supported in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
, so by (
7.13
)
h-\mathcal{T}h_{w}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. In particular

h-\mathcal{T}h_{w}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})).

(7.25)

Using (
7.24
), (
7.25
) and the assumption (
7.20
),
\big[\varphi\mapsto-\delta\mathfrak{T}[\varphi]+(h-\mathcal{T}h_{w})+\mathcal{T}(g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}})\big]
is a contraction map in both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
. Since (
7.23
) has a unique fixed point in both spaces, and
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
is a fixed point to (
7.23
), we conclude that
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
. Thus
h_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
. This finishes the proof of Claim 1.

Claim 2:

a\cdot g_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon-1}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
for some
\varepsilon>0
, and therefore
\mathcal{T}(a\cdot g_{\bar{w}})\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
.

Let
(\psi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution for
\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
. By Lemma
2.24
, for
l\geq 0
, on
\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
,

\displaystyle\psi_{l}\ast_{\theta}(ag_{\bar{w}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{l}\ast_{\theta}\Big(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3;|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\Big)(\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a)\cdot(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}})=:P_{l}^{1}+P_{l}^{2}+P_{l}^{3}.

Our goal is to show that for
\varepsilon=\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}>0
,
\sup_{l\geq 0;\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}2^{l\beta}\|P_{l}^{\nu}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon-1}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}<\infty
for
\nu=1,2,3
.

By assumption
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
and
g_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\subset\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\beta}{2}}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
, so we have
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\lesssim 2^{-j\beta}
and
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\lesssim 2^{-j^{\prime}\frac{\beta}{2}}
. Thus by Lemma
7.6

(ii)
, we have for each
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}
,

\displaystyle\|P^{1}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\lesssim_{a,g}\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}2^{-j\beta}2^{-j^{\prime}\frac{\beta}{2}}\lesssim 2^{-l\beta};

\displaystyle\|P^{3}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\lesssim_{a,g}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}2^{-j\beta}2^{-j^{\prime}\frac{\beta}{2}}\lesssim 2^{-l\frac{3}{2}\beta}.

Taking supremum over
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}
we get
\sup_{l\geq 0;\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}2^{l\beta}(\|P^{1}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}+\|P^{3}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}})<\infty
, bounding the first and the third term.

For the second sum we use
(\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a)\cdot(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}})=(\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}a))\cdot(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}wg_{\bar{w}})=(\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}a))\cdot(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}h_{\bar{w}})
. By Proposition
7.9

(ii)

\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}a)(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\frac{\alpha+4}{2}}}\lesssim 2^{-j\frac{\beta}{2}}
, by Claim 1
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}h_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}}\lesssim 2^{-j^{\prime}\beta}
. And since the product map
L^{\frac{\alpha+4}{2}}\times L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}\to L^{\frac{4(\alpha+4)}{\alpha+8}}
is bounded, we have

\displaystyle\|P^{2}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\frac{4(\alpha+4)}{\alpha+8}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}a)(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\frac{\alpha+4}{2}}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}h_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}}\lesssim\sum_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}2^{-j\frac{\beta}{2}}2^{-j^{\prime}\beta}\lesssim 2^{-l\beta}.

By Sobolev embedding
L^{\frac{4(\alpha+4)}{\alpha+8}}(\mathbb{C}^{1})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}-1}(\mathbb{C}^{1})
(also see
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.7.1]
), we conclude that
\sup_{l\geq 0;\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}2^{l\beta}\|P^{2}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}-1}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}<\infty
.

Since
\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}<\frac{\alpha}{2}
, we conclude that
ag_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. By Lemma
7.7

(i)
we get
\mathcal{T}(a\cdot g_{\bar{w}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\subset L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
, finishing the proof of Claim 2.

Final Step:

We use
\mathcal{T}(a\cdot g_{\bar{w}})\in L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
to obtain a contradiction.

Now rewriting the first equation in (
7.22
) we have

g=-\mathcal{T}(ag_{\bar{w}})+(g-\mathcal{T}g_{w})+\mathcal{T}((\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f)-\mathcal{T}((\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})-\mathcal{T}(a_{\bar{w}}).

(7.26)

By Claim 2, we have
\mathcal{T}(ag_{\bar{w}})\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. By assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{w,\theta}
, we have
(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
, so
\mathcal{T}((\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f)\in\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\subset L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
as well.

Since
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{w,\theta}\subset L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
is supported in
\{|w|<\frac{1}{2}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
, by (
7.13
)
g-\mathcal{T}g_{w}\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
as well.

Let
\mu(\theta):=\tilde{\chi}(2\theta)
, we have
\mu|_{(-\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{0},\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{0})}\equiv 1
. Thus by (
7.21
), for every
(w,\theta)\in\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times(-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
, using
\mu(\chi-1)|_{\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times(-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})}=0
we have

\mathcal{T}((\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})(w,\theta)=\mu(\theta)\mathcal{T}((\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})(w,\theta)=\mathcal{T}(\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})(w,\theta)=\big(\mathcal{T}(\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})-\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}}\big)(w,\theta).

By assumption
\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}}
is supported in
\{\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}<|w|<\varepsilon_{0}\}\times(-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
, so by Proposition
7.9

(i)
we have
\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}}\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. Using Lemma
7.7

(ii)
we get
\mathcal{T}((\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
as well.

However, by Proposition
7.9

(iii)
we have
\mathcal{T}(a_{\bar{w}})\notin L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
near
(0,0)
. Therefore near
(w,\theta)=(0,0)
, the left hand side of (
7.26
) is in
L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
while the right hand side is not, this contradicts to the assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
and finishes the proof.
∎

To summarize this subsection, what we obtained is the following:

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta>0
, let
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2}_{w},\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta};\mathbb{C})
be as in Theorem
7.11
(see (
7.15
)). Let
\mathcal{S}:=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}+a(w,\theta)\partial_{\bar{w}})
be a rank 1 complex Frobenius structure in
\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
.

Then for any neighborhood
U\times V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
of
(0,0)
there is no continuous map
F:U\times V\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
such that
F
is homeomorphic to its image,
\nabla_{w}F
and
\nabla_{z}(F^{\mathrm{Inv}})
are both continuous, and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U\times V}
.

In particular we have the result for Theorem
1.3
: if
\alpha=\beta>1
, then there is no
C^{1}
-coordinate chart
F:U\times V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
near
(0,0)
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U\times V}
.

Proof.

Suppose, by contrast, such continuous map
F
exists. Then by Proposition
7.3

(A)

\Rightarrow

(B)
there are a neighborhood
U_{2}\times V_{2}\subseteq U\times V
of
(0,0)
and a function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
that solves the PDE
f_{w}+af_{\bar{w}}=-a_{\bar{w}}
. However by Theorem
7.11
such
f
would not exist, contradiction.

When
\alpha=\beta>1
, a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart
F
implies
\nabla_{w}F\in C^{0}
and
\nabla_{z}(F^{\mathrm{Inv}})\in C^{0}
automatically, so the assumptions of Proposition
7.3

(A)
are satisfied. By Proposition
7.3

(A)

\Rightarrow

(B)
and Theorem
7.11
the proof follows.
∎

Appendix A
Some Results with non
C^{1}
Parameters

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
0<\beta<\min(\frac{2\alpha+1}{\alpha+1},\frac{3}{2})
. Let
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x},\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s};\mathbb{R}^{n}_{y})
be a map such that
F(\cdot,s)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image and
F(\mathbb{B}^{n},s)\supseteq\mathbb{B}^{n}
for each
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
. Then
\Phi(y,s):=F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y)
satisfies
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x},\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s};\mathbb{R}^{n}_{y})
.

Moreover, for any
M>1
there is a
K_{4}=K_{4}(n,q,\alpha,\beta,M)>0
that does not depend on
F
, such that if
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<M
and
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}|\det(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|>M^{-1}
then
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{4}
.

The result is indeed true for all
0<\beta\leq\alpha+1
. When
\beta>1
, the proof is simpler by applying the Inverse Function Theorem on the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-map
(x,s)\mapsto(F(x,s),s)
. In our case, we deal with the difference quotiences directly.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the quantitative result: by assumption,
\|F(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}<M
and
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\det(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|>M^{-1}
, for each
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
. Applying
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.9]
we get
\|\Phi(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{n,\alpha,M}1
.

Thus to prove the proposition, it suffices to prove
\|\Phi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta,M}1
uniformly in
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume
\alpha<1
from now on, which means
\frac{2\alpha+1}{\alpha+1}<\frac{3}{2}
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
. For
j=0,1
, applying mean value theorem to
F(\cdot,s_{j})
,

\Phi(y,s_{0})-\Phi(y,s_{1})=\nabla_{x}F\big((1-\theta_{j})\Phi(y,s_{0})+\theta_{j}\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{j}\big)^{-1}\cdot\big(F(\Phi(y,s_{0}),s_{j})-F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{j}))\big),\text{ for some }\theta_{j}\in(0,1).

(A.1)

Now
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By Lemma
2.18
, we have
\nabla_{x}F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
with
\|\nabla_{x}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}}_{x,s}}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}}\leq M
. By
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.7]
and the cofactor representation of
(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}
, we can find a
C_{1}=C_{1}(n,q,\alpha,\beta,M)>0
such that

\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|\nabla_{x}F(x,\cdot)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<C_{1},

(A.2)

when the assumptions
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<M
and
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\det(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|>M^{-1}
are satisfied.

We separate the discussions for
\beta<1
and
\beta>1
.

Case
\beta\in(0,1)
: It suffices to show
\sup_{y,s_{0},s_{1}}|\Phi(y,s_{0})-\Phi(y,s_{1})|\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta,M}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}
.

We have
y=F(\Phi(y,s_{0}),s_{0})=F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{1})
since
\Phi(\cdot,s_{j})=F(\cdot,s_{j})^{\mathrm{Inv}}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\textstyle|\Phi(y,s_{0})-\Phi(y,s_{1})|\leq\big(\sup_{\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}|(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}|\big)\cdot|F(\Phi(y,s_{0}),s_{0})-F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{0}))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textstyle C_{1}|F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{1})-F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{0}))|\leq C_{1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|F(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}\leq C_{1}M|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}.

This complete the proof of
0<\beta<1
.

Case
\beta\in(1,\frac{2\alpha+1}{\alpha+1}]
: It suffices to show
\sup_{y,s_{0},s_{1}}|\Phi(y,s_{0})+\Phi(y,s_{1})-2\Phi(y,\frac{s_{0}+s_{1}}{2})|\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta,M}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}
.

Fix a
\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. For
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
we denote
s_{\frac{1}{2}}:=\frac{s_{0}+s_{1}}{2}
,
x_{i}:=\Phi(\tilde{y},s_{i})
for
i=0,\frac{1}{2},1
.

Take
\varepsilon=1-\frac{\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}\beta
, so
0<\varepsilon<1-\frac{\beta}{\alpha+1}
. Since
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,1-\varepsilon}_{x,s}}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}}\leq M
we have
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,1-\varepsilon}_{x,s}}\leq\tilde{M}
for some
\tilde{M}=\tilde{M}(n,q,\alpha,M)>0
. Let
C_{2}:=K_{4}(n,q,\alpha,1-\varepsilon,\tilde{M})
. By the proven case
\beta=1-\varepsilon
above we have
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|\Phi(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{2}
. In particular
|x_{i}-x_{j}|=|\Phi(\tilde{y},s_{i})-\Phi(\tilde{y},s_{j})|\leq C_{2}\cdot|s_{i}-s_{j}|^{1-\varepsilon}
.

By the mean value theorem we can find
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
contained in the triangle with vertices
x_{0},x_{\frac{1}{2}},x_{1}
such that,

F(x_{0},s_{0})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{0})=\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})\cdot(x_{0}-x_{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad F(x_{1},s_{1})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1})=\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{1},s_{1})\cdot(x_{1}-x_{\frac{1}{2}}).

Clearly
|\xi_{0}-\xi_{1}|\leq\max_{i,j=0,\frac{1}{2},1}|x_{i}-x_{j}|
. Therefore by (
A.2
) and the property
|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq C_{2}\cdot|s_{i}-s_{j}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}
,

|\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})^{-1}-\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{1},s_{1})^{-1}|\leq C_{1}(|\xi_{0}-\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}+|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}})\leq C_{1}(C_{2}^{\alpha}+1)|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}.

(A.3)

Here we use the fact that
|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}}=|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}
and
|\xi_{0}-\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}\leq C_{2}^{\alpha}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\alpha(1-\varepsilon)}\leq C_{2}^{\alpha}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}
.

Therefore using
\tilde{y}=F(x_{j},s_{j})
for
j=0,\frac{1}{2},1
, we have

\displaystyle x_{0}+x_{1}-2x_{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})^{-1}\cdot(F(x_{0},s_{0})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{0}))+\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{1},s_{1})^{-1}\cdot(F(x_{1},s_{1})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1}))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})^{-1}\cdot(2\tilde{y}-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{0})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1}))+\big(\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})^{-1}-\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{1},s_{1})^{-1}\big)\cdot(\tilde{y}-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1})).

Applying (
A.2
) and (
A.3
) to the above equality:

\displaystyle|x_{0}+x_{1}-2x_{\frac{1}{2}}|\leq C_{1}|2F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{\frac{1}{2}})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{0})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1})|+C_{1}(C_{2}^{\alpha}+1)|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}\cdot|F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{\frac{1}{2}})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1})|

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{q,\beta}C_{1}\|F\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}+C_{1}(C_{2}^{\alpha}+1)\|F\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon+1-\varepsilon}\leq C_{1}(M+(C_{2}^{\alpha}+1)\tilde{M})|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}.

Thus
\sup_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|\Phi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta,M}1
and we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\alpha>1
,
\beta\in(0,\alpha]
, and let
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
,
V_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two open sets. Let
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{0},V_{0};\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a vector field on
U_{0}
with parameter on
V_{0}
.

Then for any
U_{1}\times V_{1}\Subset U_{0}\times V_{0}
there is a
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a unique map
\Phi(t,x,s):(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})\times U_{1}\times V_{1}\to U_{0}\times V_{0}
such that
\Phi(\cdot,\cdot,s)
is
C^{1}
for each
s\in V_{1}
;
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}(t,x,s)=X(\Phi(t,x,s),s)
and
\Phi(0,x,s)=x
in the domain. Moreover
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha,\beta}((-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}),U_{1},V_{1};U_{0})
.

Proof.

Take
\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})
. For each
s\in V_{1}
,
X(\cdot,s)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector field. By Lemma
3.22
,
\exp_{X(\cdot,s)}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}((-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}),U_{1};U_{0})
is the unique
C^{1}
-map
\phi(\cdot,s)
such that
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\phi(\cdot,s)=X(\cdot,s)\circ\phi(\cdot,s)
and
\phi(0,x,s)=x
. Thus, our map
\Phi(t,x,s)
is pointwisely defined and
C^{1}
in
(t,x)
. By the same argument in the proof of Lemma
3.22
we see that
\sup_{s}\|\Phi(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}}<\infty
.

When
\beta>1
,
(X(x,s),s)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-vector field on
U_{0}\times V_{0}
and we see that
(\Phi(t,x,s),s)
is its ODE flow, so by Lemma
3.22
we have
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{t,(x,s)}
. Since we already have
\sup_{s}\|\Phi(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}}<\infty
, we get
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha,\beta}_{t,x,s}
.

When
\beta<1
, by shrinking
U_{0}\times V_{0}
if necessary, we can assume
\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{0},V_{0})}<\infty
. We fix
x\in U_{1}
and
s_{0},s_{1}\in V_{1}
. Define
\psi_{j}(t):=\Phi(t,x,s_{j})
for
j=0,1
and
0\leq t<\varepsilon_{0}
, we see that

\displaystyle\textstyle|\psi_{0}(t)-\psi_{1}(t)|\leq\int_{0}^{t}|X(\psi_{0}(\tau),s_{0})-X(\psi_{1}(\tau),s_{1})|d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textstyle t\sup_{x\in U_{0}}\|X(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V_{1})}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}+\sup_{s\in V_{1}}\|X(\cdot,s)\|_{C^{0,1}(U_{0})}\int_{0}^{t}|\psi_{0}(\tau)-\psi_{1}(\tau)|d\tau.

By Gronwall’s inequality we conclude that
|\psi_{0}(t)-\psi_{1}(t)|\lesssim_{\|X\|,\varepsilon_{0}}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}
for all
0\leq t<\varepsilon_{0}
, where the implied constant does not depend on
x,s_{0},s_{1}
. Replacing
t
by
-t
, we see that
|\psi_{0}(t)-\psi_{1}(t)|\lesssim_{\|X\|,\varepsilon_{0}}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}
is also true for
-\varepsilon_{0}<t\leq 0
.
Therefore
\sup_{t,x}\|\Phi(t,x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}<\infty
and we get
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha,\beta}_{t,x,s}
.

When
\beta=1
, without loss of generality we assume
\alpha<2
since we only need to show
\sup_{t,x}\|\Phi(t,x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1}}<\infty
. We similarly fix
x\in U_{1}
,
s_{0},s_{1}\in V_{1}
. We define additionally
s_{\frac{1}{2}}:=\frac{s_{0}+s_{1}}{2}
,
\psi_{\frac{1}{2}}(t):=\Phi(t,x,\frac{s_{0}+s_{1}}{2})
and
X_{ij}(t):=X(\psi_{i}(t),s_{j})
for
i,j=0,\frac{1}{2},1
and
-\varepsilon_{0}<t<\varepsilon_{0}
. Therefore for
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\big|\psi_{0}(t)+\psi_{1}(t)-2\psi_{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\big|\leq\int_{0}^{t}|X_{00}(\tau)+X_{11}(\tau)-2X_{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)|d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\big|\tfrac{X_{00}(\tau)+X_{01}(\tau)}{2}-X_{0\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)\big|d\tau+\int_{0}^{t}\big|\tfrac{X_{10}(\tau)+X_{11}(\tau)}{2}-X_{1\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)\big|d\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}|X_{0\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)+X_{1\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)-2X_{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)|d\tau+\int_{0}^{t}\big|\tfrac{X_{00}(\tau)-X_{01}(\tau)}{2}-\tfrac{X_{10}(\tau)-X_{11}(\tau)}{2}\big|d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2t\sup_{x\in U_{0}}\|X(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1}_{s}(V_{1})}|s_{0}-s_{1}|+\sup_{s\in V_{1}}\|X(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1}_{x}(U_{0})}\int_{0}^{t}\big|\psi_{0}(\tau)+\psi_{1}(\tau)-2\psi_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)\big|d\tau

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}(U_{0},V_{1})}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}|\psi_{0}(\tau)-\psi_{1}(\tau)|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}d\tau.

By Remark
2.7

(vi)
we have
\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}(U_{0},V_{1})}\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{0}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{1}}\lesssim\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,1}_{x,s}}
. By the proven case
\beta=\frac{1}{\alpha}<1
we have
|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}|\psi_{0}(\tau)-\psi_{1}(\tau)|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}d\tau\leq|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}t\sup_{t,x}\|\Phi(t,x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\alpha}}\lesssim_{\alpha,X,\varepsilon_{0}}|s_{0}-s_{1}|
. Therefore
\big|\psi_{0}(t)+\psi_{1}(t)-2\psi_{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\big|\leq C_{X,\alpha,\varepsilon_{0}}(|s_{0}-s_{1}|+\int_{0}^{t}\big|\psi_{0}(\tau)+\psi_{1}(\tau)-2\psi_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau)\big|d\tau)
for some
C_{X,\alpha,\varepsilon_{0}}
that does not depend on
x,s_{0},s_{1}
. Thus by Gronwall’s inequality again we get
\sup_{t,x}\|\Phi(t,x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1}}<\infty
, finishing the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Holomorphic Extension of Laplacian

In this section, for a holomorphic function
f(z)
we use
\nabla f=\partial_{z}f=(\partial_{1}f,\dots,\partial_{n}f)
(cf. (
1.2
)).

We use
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n-1}
for unit sphere.
We recall
\mathbb{H}^{n}=\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:|x|<1,4|y|<1-|x|\}
from (
4.14
). We use
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
for the space of holomorphic functions in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, endowed with compact-open topology.

Our goal in this section is to prove Proposition
4.8
. The idea follows from Morrey
[
Mor58
]
, which only consider the cases for positive
\alpha
.
By taking direct modification to our construction (see (
B.9
)) we can show that there exist an inverse Laplacian
\mathbf{P}
and its extension
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
such that
\mathbf{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
is bounded for all
-M<\alpha<M
, where
M
is an arbitrarily fixed large number.

We illustrate some property of holomorphic Hölder space in Section
B.1
. We construct the inverse Laplacian
\mathbf{P}
in Definition
B.5
and its holomorphic extension
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
in Definition
B.9
. We decompose the proof of Proposition
4.8
into Propositions
B.2
,
B.6
,
B.10
and
B.12
.

In this section we use
\mathbf{\Gamma}
as the Newtonian potential in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, namely

\mathbf{\Gamma}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{|x|}{2}&n=1,\\
\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|&n=2,\\
\frac{|x|^{2-n}}{(2-n)|\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|}&n\geq 3,\end{cases}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}.

(B.1)

Note that
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
is contained in
\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:\operatorname{Re}(z\cdot z)>0\}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:|\operatorname{Re}z|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\}
, which is a connect set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. So
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}\mapsto|x|
has unique holomorphic extension to
\{|\operatorname{Re}z|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\}
as

z\in\big\{|\operatorname{Re}z|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\big\}\mapsto(z\cdot z)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\text{with principle argument }-\tfrac{\pi}{2}<2\arg z<\tfrac{\pi}{2}.

(B.2)

We still use
\mathbf{\Gamma}
as the holomorphic extended fundamental solution on
\{|\operatorname{Re}z|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\}
.

Therefore, for each
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
, the function
[x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}(x-\theta)]
can be holomorphic extended to
[z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-\theta)]
. Moreover, for every multindex
\nu\neq 0
, since
|\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma}(x)|\lesssim_{\nu}|x|^{2-n-|\nu|}
, we have the following

\displaystyle|(\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma})(z-t)|\lesssim_{\nu}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{2-n-|\nu|}

\displaystyle,\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}^{n},z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\text{ satisfying }|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq 2|\operatorname{Im}z|.

(B.3)

B.1
Holomorphic Hölder spaces and extension of harmonic functions

For holomorphic functions in a complex domain whose Hölder norms are finite, we can use the blow-up speed of its derivative near the boundary.

Let
\alpha<1
, recall in Definition
4.11
that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
is the space of all holomorphic functions in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
such that
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}=|f(0)|+\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\mathbb{H})^{1-\alpha}|\nabla f(z)|<\infty
.

We have in fact
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, see
[
Zhu05
, Chapter 7]
for some illustrations. For completeness we prove some partial results that are used later.

Lemma B.1

(Hardy-Littlewood type characterizations)
.

(i)

Let
\alpha<1
. The differentiation
[f\mapsto\nabla f]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
is bounded linear.

(ii)

Let
\alpha<0
. Then
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
has equivalent norm

f\mapsto\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|
.

(iii)

The restriction map
[f\mapsto f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{C})
and the inclusion map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
are both bounded linear for
0<\alpha<1
.

(iv)

Let
\alpha<1
. If
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
satisfies
\Delta g=0
, then
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathbb{S}^{n-1})^{1-\alpha}|\nabla g(x)|<\infty
. Moreover there is a
C=C(n,\alpha)>0
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathbb{S}^{n-1})^{1-\alpha}|\nabla g(x)|\leq C_{3}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})},\quad\forall g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\cap\ker\Delta.

Proof.

(i)
: By assumption
|\partial_{j}f(z)|\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha-1}
. By Cauchy’s integral formula

\partial_{j}g(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi r}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-i\theta}g(z+re^{i\theta}\mathbf{e}_{j})d\theta,\quad 1\leq j\leq n,\quad 0<r<\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}),\quad g\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n}).

(B.4)

So take
r=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
and
g=\partial_{k}f
, we get
|\partial_{jk}f(z)|\leq\frac{1}{r}\sup_{|w-z|=r}|\partial_{k}f(w)|\leq 2^{2-\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha-2}
, for
1\leq j,k\leq n
, which means
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{2-\alpha}|\nabla^{2}f(z)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}
. This proves
(i)
.

(ii)
: Now
\alpha<0
. Clearly
|f(0)|\leq\operatorname{dist}(0,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}\cdot\sup_{z}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|
. If
\sup_{z}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|<\infty
, then by (
B.4
) with
g=f
and
r=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
we get
|\nabla f(z)|\leq\frac{1}{r}\sup_{|w-z|=r}|f(w)|\lesssim\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}
. So
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\lesssim\sup_{z}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|
.

Conversely since
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is a convex set, we have
f(z)=f(0)+\int_{0}^{1}z\cdot\nabla f(tz)dt
for all
f\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
and
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Note that
\operatorname{dist}(tz,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\geq t\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})+(1-t)\operatorname{dist}(0,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
for all
0\leq t\leq 1
. Therefore for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
,

\displaystyle|f(z)|

\displaystyle\leq|f(0)|+|z|\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{0}^{1}\big(t\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})+(1-t)\operatorname{dist}(0,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\big)^{\alpha-1}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}+\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{0}^{1-\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})}(1-t)^{\alpha-1}dt\lesssim_{\alpha}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}.

So
\sup_{z}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}
, completing the proof of
(ii)
.

(iii)
: Now
0<\alpha<1
. It suffices to show
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
. The boundedness of the inclusion and restriction would then follow from the natural maps
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
respectively.

Now for
z_{0},z_{1}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, what we need is to show that
|f(z_{0})-f(z_{1})|\lesssim_{n,\alpha}|z_{0}-z_{1}|^{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})}
.

Since
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is convex, by its geometry we can find a point
w\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
(depending on
z_{0},z_{1}
) such that
|w-z_{j}|\leq|z_{0}-z_{1}|
and
\operatorname{dist}(tw+(1-t)z_{j},\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\geq\frac{1}{100}t|z_{0}-z_{1}|
for
j=0,1
and
0\leq t\leq 1
. Therefore

\displaystyle|f(z_{0})-f(z_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq|f(z_{0})-f(w)|+|f(z_{1})-f(w)|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}|z_{j}-w||\nabla f(tw+(1-t)z_{j})|dt

\displaystyle\lesssim|z_{0}-z_{1}|\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{0}^{1}(|z_{0}-z_{1}|t)^{\alpha-1}dt\lesssim|z_{0}-z_{1}|^{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}.

Thus
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
and we finish the proof of
(iii)
.

(iv)
: Let
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
be a radial function such that
\int\rho=1
. Let
\rho_{j}(x):=2^{jn}\rho(2^{j}x)
for
j\geq 0
. Therefore, for a harmonic function
g
in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, by the ball average formula we have
g(x)=\rho_{k}\ast g(x)
whenever
1-|x|>2^{-k}
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution (see Definition
2.1
) for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
\nabla\rho
is Schwartz for every
M>0
we have
\|\phi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{\phi,\rho,M}2^{-Mj}
for
j\geq 0
, so by scaling, we get
\|\phi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=2^{k}\|\phi_{j-k}\ast\nabla\rho\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{\phi,\rho,M}2^{k-M(j-k)}
if
j\geq k
. Therefore
\|\phi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{\phi,\rho,M}2^{k}\min(1,2^{-M(j-k)})
for all
j,k\geq 0
.

Let
\psi_{j}:=\sum_{k=j-1}^{j+1}\phi_{j}
for
j\geq 0
(we use
\phi_{-1}:=0
). By (
2.1
) we have
\psi_{j}\ast\phi_{j}=\phi_{j}
. Therefore

\|\psi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{M}2^{k}\min(1,2^{-M(j-k)}),\quad j,k\geq 0.

Now for a harmonic function
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, consider an extension
\tilde{g}:=E_{x}g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
where
E_{x}
is in Lemma
2.8
. By Lemma
2.2

(i)
(if
0<\alpha<1
) and Definition
2.4
(if
\alpha\leq 0
) we have
\|\phi_{k}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{\phi,E_{x}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}2^{-k\alpha}
.
Thus, for any
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, take
k\geq 0
to be the unique integer such that
1-2^{1-k}\leq|x|<1-2^{-k}
, we get

\displaystyle|\nabla g(x)|=|\nabla\rho_{k}\ast g(x)|\leq\|\nabla\rho_{k}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\ast\psi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\psi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\ast\phi_{j}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\psi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\|\phi_{j}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha,\phi}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{k}\min(1,2^{-(|\alpha|+1)(j-k)})2^{-k\alpha}\|\tilde{g}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}2^{k(1-\alpha)}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

We conclude that
|\nabla g(x)|\lesssim(1-|x|)^{\alpha-1}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
for
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, finishing the proof of
(iv)
.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
4.8

(iii)
. Recall that for a real analytic function
f
, we use
\mathbf{E}f
to be the (unique) holomorphic extension to the complex domain where it is defined.

Proposition B.2
.

The holomorphic extension

\mathbf{E}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\cap\ker\Delta\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
is defined and bounded for
-2<\alpha<1
.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
be such that
\Delta f=0
. By Lemma
B.1

(iv)

f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, and by Poisson’s formula (See
[
Eva10
, Chapter 2.2 (45)]
for example)

f(x)=\frac{r^{2}-|x-x_{0}|^{2}}{|r\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|}\int_{\partial B(x_{0},r)}\frac{f(\theta)d\sigma(\theta)}{|x-\theta|^{n}},\quad\text{for }x_{0}\in\mathbb{B}^{n},\quad 0<r<1-|x_{0}|,\quad x\in B^{n}(x_{0},r).

Here
d\sigma
is the standard spherical measure.

Using (
B.2
) we have holomorphic extension,

\mathbf{E}f(z)=\frac{r^{2}-(z-x_{0})^{2}}{|r\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|}\int_{\partial B(x_{0},r)}\frac{f(\theta)d\sigma(\theta)}{((z-\theta)^{2})^{\frac{n}{2}}},\quad x_{0}\in\mathbb{B}^{n},\quad r<1-|x_{0}|,\quad|\operatorname{Im}z|<r-|\operatorname{Re}z-x_{0}|.

For
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, take
x_{0}=\operatorname{Re}z
and
r=\frac{1-|x_{0}|}{2}
, we can write

\mathbf{E}f(x+iy)=\frac{(\frac{1-|x|}{2})^{2}+y^{2}}{|\frac{1-|x|}{2}\cdot\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|}\int_{\frac{1-|x|}{2}\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\frac{f(x+\theta)d\sigma(\theta)}{((iy-\theta)^{2})^{\frac{n}{2}}},\quad x+iy\in\mathbb{H}^{n}.

(B.5)

Since
|y|<\frac{1}{4}(1-|x|)=\frac{1}{2}|\theta|
for
\theta\in\frac{1-|x|}{2}\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, we have
\operatorname{Re}((iy-\theta)^{2})\approx|\theta|^{2}\approx(1-|x|)^{2}
.

Note that
\nabla f
is also harmonic, which gives
\nabla_{z}\mathbf{E}f=\mathbf{E}(\nabla_{x}f)
. Therefore by Lemma
B.1

(iv)
,

|\nabla\mathbf{E}f(x+iy)|\lesssim\frac{(1-|x|)^{2}}{1-|x|}\int_{\frac{1-|x|}{2}\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\frac{|\nabla f(x+\theta)|d\sigma(\theta)}{(1-|x|)^{n}}\lesssim\sup\limits_{\partial B^{n}(x,\frac{1-|x|}{2})}|\nabla f|\lesssim(1-|x|)^{\alpha-1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

Since
\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\geq\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\approx 1-|x|
, we get
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{1-\alpha}|\nabla\mathbf{E}f(z)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
.

Clearly
|\mathbf{E}f(0)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
, so by definition of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}
-spaces we get
\|\mathbf{E}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
, finishing the proof.
∎

B.2
Construction and Hölder regularity for real inverse Laplacian
\mathbf{P}

We define
\mathbf{P}f=(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
where
E
is a concrete extension operator for
\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that
E:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded linear for
-4<\alpha<1
.

Lemma B.3

(
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.9.2]
)
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, let
(a_{j},b_{j})_{j=1}^{M}\subset\mathbb{R}
satisfy
b_{j}>0
,
1\leq j\leq M
and
\sum_{j=1}^{M}a_{j}(-b_{j})^{k}=1
for
-4\leq k\leq 4
. Let
(\tilde{\chi}_{j})_{j=1}^{M}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\chi_{j}(t)\equiv 1
for
t
closed to 0 and each
1\leq j\leq M
. Define

E^{H}f(x^{\prime},x_{n}):=\begin{cases}f(x^{\prime},x_{n})&x_{n}>0,\\
\sum_{j=1}^{M}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(x_{n})a_{j}\cdot f(x^{\prime},-b_{j}x_{n})&x_{n}<0.\end{cases}

(B.6)

Then
E^{H}
defines an extension operator on the half plane
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}=\{x_{n}>0\}
such that
E^{H}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
-4<\alpha<1
.

Corollary B.4
.

Let
(a_{j},b_{j})_{j=1}^{9}\subset\mathbb{R}
satisfy
b_{j}>0
,
1\leq j\leq 9
and
\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}(-b_{j})^{-k}=1
for
-4\leq k\leq 4
. For
1\leq j\leq 9
, let
\tilde{\chi}_{j}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\tilde{\chi}_{j}\equiv 1
in an neighborhood of
0
.
Then

Ef(e^{-\rho}\theta):=\begin{cases}f(e^{-\rho}\theta)&\rho>0,\\
\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(\rho)f(e^{\rho/b_{j}}\theta)&\rho<0,\end{cases}\quad\rho\in\mathbb{R},\quad\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1},

(B.7)

defines an extension operator on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that
E:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded linear for
-4<\alpha<1
.

Moreover its formal adjoint
E^{*}
is given by

E^{*}g(x)=g(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|x|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(b_{j}\log|x|)g\Big(\frac{x}{|x|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big),\quad x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.

(B.8)

Proof.

Clearly
(\rho,\theta)\mapsto e^{-\rho}\theta
is a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
and maps
\{\rho>0\}
onto
\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
. By
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.10.2(i)]
we see that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}
is preserved by bounded diffeomorphism. Therefore, by passing to coordinate covers of
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
if necessary (since we only need to worry about the place where
\rho
is closed to
0\in\mathbb{R}
), by Lemma
B.3
with
b_{j}
replacing by
\frac{1}{b_{j}}
, we know (
B.7
) is an extension operator for
\mathbb{B}^{n}
and has
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-boundedness for
-4<\alpha<1
.

To see (
B.8
), let
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}Ef(y)g(y)dt=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}d\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}}Ef(e^{-\rho}\theta)g(e^{-\rho}\theta)e^{-n\rho}d\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}f(e^{-\rho}\theta)g(e^{-\rho}\theta)e^{-n\rho}d\rho+\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(\rho)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{-}}f(e^{\rho/b_{j}}\theta)g(e^{-\rho}\theta)e^{-n\rho}d\rho\bigg)d\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}d\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}f(e^{-r}\theta)g(e^{-r}\theta)e^{-nr}dr+\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}b_{j}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}d\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(b_{j}r)f(e^{-r}\theta)g(e^{b_{j}r}\theta)e^{nr(b_{j}+1)}e^{-nr}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f(x)\bigg(g(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}\cdot\tilde{\chi}_{j}(b_{j}\log|x|)}{|x|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}g\Big(\frac{x}{|x|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg)dx.

This gives (
B.8
) and finishes the proof.
∎

We now give an concrete formula of
\mathbf{P}
based on (
B.8
),

Definition B.5
.

We define
\mathbf{P}
be

\mathbf{P}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f(t)\bigg(\mathbf{\Gamma}(x-t)+\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\Big(x-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg)dt.

(B.9)

Here
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{3},\frac{5}{3})
satisfies
\chi|_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}]}\equiv 1
, and
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R},b_{j}>0
satisfy
\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}(-b_{j})^{k}=1
for
-4\leq k\leq 4
.

Thus we have Proposition
4.8

(i)
almost immediately.

Proposition B.6
.

Let
\mathbf{P}
defined as in (
B.9
), then
\mathbf{P}
is an left inverse of Laplacian such that
\mathbf{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is bounded for
-4<\alpha<-1
.

Proof.

By (
B.8
) we see that
\mathbf{P}f
is of the form
\mathbf{P}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f(t)E^{*}_{t}\mathbf{\Gamma}(x-t)dt
for
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, where
E^{*}
is the adjoint of some extension operator
E
which is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-bounded for
-4<\alpha<1
. Therefore
\mathbf{P}f(x)=(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and we get

\mathbf{P}f=(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
. In particular
\Delta\mathbf{P}f=(\Delta\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=f
holds.

By construction, such
E
has compact support, namely there is a bounded open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
(in fact we can take
U=B^{n}\big(0,e^{\max_{j}\frac{1}{3b_{j}}}\big)
) such that
\operatorname{supp}Ef\subset U
has compact support for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
. So
\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef
is a well-defined distribution.

Since
\Delta(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=Ef|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=f
, the classical interior regularity for elliptic equations (see
[
Tay11
, Proposition 4.1]
for example) shows that if
Ef\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
, then
\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
. So
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
implies
\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
. By the Closed Graph Theorem
\mathbf{P}=[f\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is bounded linear.
∎

We decompose the integral in (
B.9
) into the domains
\{t:|\operatorname{Re}z-t|<\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}\}
and
\{t:|\operatorname{Re}z-t|>\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}\}
. We need the following estimate.

Lemma B.7
.

Let
a,b,\chi
be given in Definition
B.5
. There is a
C=C(n,a,b,\chi)>0
, such that for every
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
and
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
satisfying
|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}
,

\bigg|(\nabla\mathbf{\Gamma})(z-t)+\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}(\nabla\mathbf{\Gamma})\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg|\leq C|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-n-3}(1-|t|)^{4}.

(B.10)

Proof.

That
|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}
implies
|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq 2|\operatorname{Im}z|
, so by (
B.3
) the left hand side of (
B.10
) is finite.

When
1-|t|\geq\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
, we have
|\operatorname{Re}z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}|\geq\frac{1}{b_{j}}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
. Thus by (
B.3
),

\textstyle\text{LHS of }\eqref{Eqn::SecHolLap::AprioriBddforPEqn}\lesssim|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{1-n}+|\operatorname{Re}z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}|^{1-n}\lesssim_{n,b}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{1-n}\lesssim|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-3-n}(1-|t|)^{4}.

We then focus on the case
1-|t|<\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
.

By assumption
\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}b_{j}(-b_{j})^{k}=-1
for
0\leq k\leq 3
. Therefore, by the Taylor’s expansion of deg 3, for any
\tilde{\chi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
that equals 1 in the neighborhood of 0, there is a
C^{\prime}=C_{a,b,\tilde{\chi}}^{\prime}>0
such that

\textstyle\big|\psi(\rho)+\tilde{\chi}(\rho)\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}b_{j}\psi(-b_{j}\rho)\big|\leq C^{\prime}\|\psi\|_{C^{4}}\cdot\rho^{4},\quad\forall\ R>0,\ \psi\in C^{4}[-R\max_{1\leq j\leq 9}b_{j},R],\ \rho\in[0,R].

(B.11)

For fixed
t
and
z
in this case, we define
I_{t}:=\big[\log|t|\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq 9}b_{j},-\log|t|\big]
and
\psi_{z,t}(\rho):=e^{-n\rho}\cdot(\nabla\mathbf{\Gamma})(z-e^{-\rho}\frac{t}{|t|})
for
\rho\in I_{t}
. Since for
\rho\in I_{t}
we have
|\operatorname{Re}z-e^{-\rho}\frac{t}{|t|}|\geq|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
and by assumption
|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq 2|\operatorname{Im}z|
holds in this case, using (
B.3
) we have
\|\psi_{z,t}\|_{C^{4}(I_{t})}\lesssim|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-n-3}
.

Thus taking
\tilde{\chi}(\rho):=\chi(e^{-\rho})
and
\rho=-\log|t|(=R)
in (
B.11
) with
\psi=\psi_{z,t}
we get

\bigg||t|^{n}(\nabla\mathbf{\Gamma})(z-t)+\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{nb_{j}}}(\nabla\mathbf{\Gamma})\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg|\lesssim|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-n-3}(1-|t|)^{4}.

Note that
1-|t|<\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
implies
\frac{1}{3}<|t|<1
, so by multiplying
\frac{1}{|t|^{n}}
we get (
B.10
) for the case
1-|t|<\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary B.8
.

If
(1-|t|)^{4}f(t)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, then the following integral converges locally uniformly for
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\{t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}\}}f(t)\Big(\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-t)-\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\big)\Big)dt.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.7
(taking an anti-derivative if necessary), for each
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
there is a
C_{z}>0
which is locally bounded in
z
, such that
